=z his Report for the year ended March 2013 has been prepared
for submission to the Governor of Uttar Pradesh under
Article 151 of the Constitution of India.

The Report contains significant results of the performance audit
and/or compliance audit of the Departments of the Government of
Uttar Pradesh under the General and Social Sector
Services/Economic Services including Departments of
Agriculture, Animal Husbandry, Medical Education and
Training, Social Welfare, Technical Education, Medical and
Health Services, Public Works, Home (Police), Housing and
Urban Planning, Irrigation, Higher Education, Rural
Development, Revenue, Vocational Education.

The instances mentioned in this Report are those, which came to
notice in the course of test audit for the period 2012-13 as well as
those which came to notice in earlier years, but could not be
reported in the previous Audit Reports; instances relating to the
period subsequent to 2012-13 have also been included, wherever
necessary.

The audit has been conducted in conformity with the Auditing
Standards issued by the Comptroller and Auditor General of
India.

1.1 About this Report

This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India (C&AG) relates
to matters arising from compliance audit of the transactions of the Civil and
Works departments of the Government of Uttar Pradesh, Centrally sponsored
and State plan schemes and audit of autonomous bodies of the State, including
performance audit of selected schemes and departments.

The primary purpose of the Report is to bring to the notice of the State
Legislature, important results of audit. Auditing standards require that the
materiality level of reporting should be commensurate with the nature, volume
and magnitude of transactions. The findings of audit are expected to enable the
Executive to take the corrective action and to frame policies and directives that
lead to improved financial management of the organisations, thus contributing
to better governance.

Compliance audit refers to examination of the transactions relating to
expenditure, receipts, assets and liabilities of the audited entities to ascertain
whether the provision of the Constitution of India, applicable laws, rules and
regulations and various orders and instructions issued by the competent
authorities are being complied with.

Performance audit is an independent assessment or examination of the extent
to which an organisation, programme or scheme operates economically,
efficiently and effectively.

This Chapter provides the audited entity profile, the planning and extent of
audit and follow-up on Audit Reports. Chapter 2 of this Report deals with the
findings of performance audit and Chapter 3 deals with compliance audit in
various departments and autonomous bodies.

1.2. Auditee profile

There are 71 government departments, headed by the Chief Secretary/
Principal Secretaries/Secretaries who are assisted by the Special Secretaries,
Deputy Secretaries and Directors and subordinates officers under them, which
are under audit jurisdiction of the Principal Accountant General (General &
Social Sector Audit), Uttar Pradesh, Allahabad.

The comparative position of expenditure of the Government during 2012-13
and in the preceding two years is given in Table 1.

1.3 Authority for audit

Authority for audit by C&AG is derived from the Articles 149 and 151 of the
Constitution of India and the Comptroller and Auditor General's (Duties,
Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971. Principal Accountant General
(General and Social Sector Audit) conducted audit of expenditure of civil and
works departments, autonomous bodies under sections 13, 14, 15, 19 and 20 of
the Comptroller & Auditor General’s (Duties, Powers and Conditions of
Service) Act, 1971. The principles and methodology for audit are prescribed in
the manuals issued by the Comptroller & Auditor General of India.

1.4 Organisational structure of the office of the Principal Accountant
General (General and Social Sector Audit), Uttar Pradesh

Under the directions of C&AG, the Principal Accountant General (General
and Social Sector Audit), Uttar Pradesh, conducts audit of civil and works
departments and autonomous bodies through five sectoral groups. During
2012-13, 204 audit parties conducted compliance audit of the selected units
under various departments of the State Government, autonomous bodies etc.

1.5 Planning and conduct of audit

Audit process commences with the assessment of risk of various Government
departments /organizations /autonomous bodies and schemes /projects etc.,
based on expenditure, criticality /complexity of activities, level of delegated
financial powers, assessment of internal control and the concerns of
stakeholders. Previous audit findings are also considered in this exercise.

After completion of audit of each unit, Inspection Reports containing audit
findings are issued to the head of the unit/department. The units are requested
to furnish replies to the audit findings within one month of receipt of the

o————


Chapter 1 - Introduction

Inspection Report. Whenever replies are received, audit findings are either
settled or further action for compliance is advised. The important audit
observations arising out of these Inspection Reports are processed for
inclusion in the Audit Report.

During 2012-13, 10,464 party-days were used to carry out audit of 1,765 units
of various departments/ organisations. The audit plan covered those units/
entities which were vulnerable to significant risk, as per the assessment.

1.6 Significant observations of Performance Audits

Performance audit is undertaken to ensure whether the Government
programmes have achieved the desired objectives at the minimum cost and the
intended benefits have accrued to the targeted beneficiaries.

This report contains results of Performance Audits on Rashtriya Krishi Vikas
Yojna, Indira Awas Yojna, Functioning of Moti Lal Nehru Medical College
Allahabad and Associated Teaching Hospitals, and long paragraphs on
Financial Management in Institute of Engineering and Rural Technology,
Allahabad and Operation of Plant and Machinery in Electrical and Mechanical
divisions of the PWD. The salient findings of the Performance Audits are
discussed below.

1.6.1 Performance Audit of ‘Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojna’

Government of India launched Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana in May 2007.
The objective was to provide incentives to the State for increasing public
investment in agriculture, maximize returns to the farmers etc. Though the
District Planning Committees were constituted the work of preparation of
District Agriculture Plans / State Agriculture Plans was outsourced to the
Agriculture Finance Corporation, Lucknow, a deemed Government of India
Company and UP Industrial Constulants Limited, Lucknow. The funds were
utilized (® 2,493.28 crore) without any District Agriculture Plans. The State
Government released funds with delays ranging between one and eleven
month. There were cases of diversion of € 28.45 crore. There were instances
of expenditure on projects which were either not eligible or were not included
in the District Agriculture Plans/State Agriculture Plans. Funds were also
utilized in National Food Security Mission (NFSM) districts though the
project was to be implemented in non-NFSM districts. The monitoring and
evaluation of the districts were not done, as envisaged.

1.6.2 Performance Audit on Implementation of Indira Awaas Yojana

Government of India launched Indira Awaas Yojana in January 1996 as an
independent scheme. The objective was to help rural BPL householders of
Scheduled Castes/Scheduled Tribes, freed bonded labourers, minorities etc. by
providing them a lump sum financial assistance in construction/upgradation of
dwelling units. However, the districts test checked did not prepare annual
plans. Consequently, targets distributed to the blocks were not as per the
criteria. The names of the beneficiaries were not found in the lists of BPL

eee


dit Report (General y ended 31 March 2013

families/ waitlist. Ineligible people were also sanctioned the houses. Further,
the houses were sanctioned in the name of the male members of the family
instead the female members. The State shares were released with delays and
the funds were diverted for construction of houses under other schemes. Due
to inadequate IEC activities in the State, the beneficiaries were not aware of
the facilities available to them under convergence.

1.6.3 Functioning of Moti Lal Nehru Medical College, Allahabad and
Associated Teaching Hospitals

The perspective and annual action plans were not prepared. The academic
activities suffered due to inadequate faculty members. The college offered the
unrecognized Post Graduate degree courses and Post Graduate diploma
Courses. The activities relating to the research and the college council etc. was
dismal as neither the College Council was constituted nor was Central
Laboratory functional to take up research activities. Further, there were
shortages of specialists, consultant doctors and nursing staff despite the fact
that the numbers of patients were increasing. The environment of the hospital
wards was unhygienic /unfavourable. The hospital beds were shared amongst
the patients. The benefit of free treatment was also not provided to those living
below the poverty line. The specialized services remained inadequate for want
of AMC of the equipment, approval of the proposal for the civil works.
Besides, fifteen departments of the college lacked equipment. There was loss
of revenue also during 2008-11 as the area for a chemist shop in the premises
of SRNH was not provided to the firm. Monitoring was inadequate and
incorrect figures were reported to the Government.

1.6.4 Financial Management in Institute of Engineering and Rural
Technology, Allahabad

There were instances of misuse and non-accountal of tuition fee in the Institute
of Engineering and Rural Technology (IERT), Allahabad. The multiple bank
accounts were maintained resulting in difference between the bank accounts
and cash book balances. Cash Books were maintained improperly. The
Director, IERT sanctioned 2,985 Temporary Imprests (TIs) amounting to
र₹ 18.71 crore during 2003-13. These TIs were either lying unadjusted or were
adjusted inexplicably and with excessive delays. Further, TIs were given to the
officials without obtaining approvals of the competent authority. TIs were
given for meeting expenditure on inadmissible items. Besides, excess fee was
also realised from the students. Reimbursement of fee of the Scheduled Castes
and Scheduled Tribe students was also claimed in excess from District Social
Welfare Officer. The amounts on account of both employee and employer's
contribution were also not transferred to the Provident Fund accounts of the
work charged staff.

1.6.5 Operation of Plant and Machinery in Electrical and Mechanical
divisions

Average performance of the departmental Hot Mix Plants was 49 per cent.

Procurement of the plant & machinery from the Depreciation Reserve Fund

was unwarranted and without any need based assessment. Further, the
recovery charges remained outstanding for over a decade. Besides, utilization
of funds from Depreciation Reserve Fund was not as per rules.

1.7 Significant observations of Compliance Audit

Audit observed significant deficiencies in critical areas, which impact the
effectiveness of the State Government. Some important findings of the
compliance audit (28 paragraphs) have also been reported. The major
observations relate to:

e Fraud and detection of fraud.

e Non-compliance with rules and regulations;

e Audit against propriety and cases of expenditure without adequate

justification; and.

e Failure of oversight/governance.

1.7.1 Fraud and detection of fraud

Audit of financial transactions, test-checked in some departments of the
Government and their field functionaries, showed instances of fraudulent
payments as discussed in the following paragraphs:

Rupees 71 lakh was fraudulently drawn from the Treasury at Rae Bareli as the
prescribed system was not adhered to in the office of the Commandant, 25
Battalion, Provincial Armed Constabulary, Rae Bareli.

(Paragraph 3.1.1)

Failure to follow financial rules and the exercise of required internal
control/checks led to fraudulent payments of salary of हैं 10.36 lakh to two
beldars due to stayal in service beyond the date(s) of superannuation.

(Paragraph 3.1.2 )

Payment of ® 4.31 crore, in violation of the financial rules, accounting system
and Government orders led to fraud and misappropriation.

(Paragraph 3.1.3 )
1.7.2. Non-compliance with rules and regulations

For sound financial administration and control, it is essential that expenditure
conforms to financial rules, regulations and orders issued by the competent
authority. This helps in maintaining financial discipline and prevents
irregularities, misappropriation and frauds. This section brings out instances of
non-compliance with rules and regulations. Some of these are as under.

Failure of the Government to observe codal provisions led to retention of
% 233.12 crore outside the Government account in a scheduled bank for 36
months already.

(Paragraph 3.2.2 )

ee


dit Report (General y ended 31 March 2013

Non-adherence to the financial rules and Government orders for levying of
centage charges led to loss of ® 13.22 crore to the Government.

(Paragraph 3.2.4 )

Purchase of high density polyethylene/polypropylene bags, in violation of
financial rules and the Government orders, led to loss of रैं 1.18 crore to the
Government.

(Paragraph 3.2.5 )

1.7.3 Audit against propriety and cases of expenditure without adequate
justification

Authorisation of expenditure from public funds is to be guided by the
principles of propriety and efficiency of public expenditure. Authorities
empowered to incur expenditure are expected to enforce the same vigilance as
a person of ordinary prudence would exercise in respect of his own money.
Audit scrutiny revealed instances of impropriety and extra expenditure. Some
of the important audit findings are as under.

Failure of the Government to withdraw its share as per scheme guidelines
with effect from 2005-06 by determining premium on actuarial basis, led to
the Government paying ® 149 crore (2008-12) as farmers’ share towards
premium (% 20 crore) and insurance claim (& 129 crore) to an insurance
company under National Agriculture Insurance Scheme.

(Paragraph 3.3.1)

Dismantling of pan shaped stone jali just after it’s fixing resulted in loss of
% 0.83 crore to the Government and misutilisation of funds to the tune of
₹ 1,98 crore.

(Paragraph 3.3.5)
1.7.4 Failure of oversight/governance

Government has an obligation to improve the quality of life of the people in
the area of health, education, development and upgradation of infrastructure,
public services, etc. Audit noticed instances where the funds released by the
Government for creating public assets, remained unutilized/ blocked or proved
unfruitful/ unproductive due to indecisiveness, lack of administrative oversight
and concerted action at various levels. Some cases of failure of
oversight/governance noticed in audit are as under.

An Expenditure of % 3.37 crore on renovation of the electric lines and power
sub-station works in Sarojini Naidu Medical College, Agra was rendered
unfruitful as the works were commenced without the requisite ‘No Objection
Certificate’ from the Electrical Safety Directorate, Lucknow.

(Paragraph 3.4.5)

Either no or negligible number of beggars were trained and rehabilitated
during 2006-13 despite expenditure of रैं 14.96 crore on salary, allowances,
rent and electricity on maintenance of eight certified institutions for beggars
as the police did not apprehend them.

1.8 Responsiveness of Government to Audit

1.8.1 Lack of responses to the draft performance reviews and audit
paragraphs

The draft performance reviews and audit paragraphs are forwarded to the
Government drawing attention to the audit findings and requesting to send
responses within six weeks. It is brought to their personal attention that in
view of likely inclusion of such paragraphs in the Audit Reports, it would be
desirable to include their comments in the matter. They are also advised to
have meetings with the Principal Accountant General to discuss the draft
performance reviews/audit paragraphs, proposed for Audit Reports.

During May to November 2013, three draft performance reviews and
30 draft paragraphs (including two long paragraphs) were forwarded to the
Government and the meetings for discussions were atranged during
August 2013 to May 2014. The responses in respect of all the draft performance
teviews were received. However, replies of the Government in respect of three
paragraphs were not received. The results of discussions and the replies of the
Government received have also been suitably incorporated.

1.8.2 Lack of response to Audit

The Principal Accountant General (G&SSA), Allahabad arranges to conduct
periodical audit inspections of the Government departments. These inspections
are followed up with Inspections Reports (IRs). A copy of each of the
paragraphs on the irregularities noticed during test check of records is sent to
the next higher authorities and the Government so as to facilitate monitoring
of the audit observations and its settlement. The Heads of offices and the next
higher authorities are required to comply with the audit observations and
rectify the defects promptly and report their compliance to the office of the
Principal Accountant General (G&SSA), Allahabad. Based on the results of
test audit, 28,650 audit observations contained in 8,183 IRs were outstanding
as on 31 March 2013.

1.8.3. Lack of response to Audit Reports

At the end of March 2013, 1,028 paragraphs/reviews were pending for
discussion in the Public Accounts Committee. These paragraphs/reviews
pertained to the period 1983-84 to 2010-11 except for the years 1997-98,
2002-03, 2004-05 and 2005-06. The discussion by the Public Accounts
Committee on paragraphs/reviews of the Audit Reports for the years 2009-10
and 2010-11 was in progress.

This chapter contains the results of performance audits of Rashtriya Krishi
Vikas Yojna; Implementation of Indira Awaas Yojna; and Functioning of Moti
Lal Nehru Medical College, Allahabad and Associated Teaching Hospitals and

Agriculture Department

2.1 Performance Audit of ‘Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojna’
Executive summary

Introduction

Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojna was launched (May 2007) with the objective to
provide incentives to the State for increasing public investment in
agriculture, maximise returns to the farmers, bring about quantifiable
changes in the production and productivity of various agriculture and allied
sectors and to ensure annual growth rate of at least four per cent during
Eleventh Five Year Plan.

Planning

District Agriculture Plans were prepared (March 2009) by Agriculture
Financial Corporation, Lucknow and UP Industrial Consultants Limited,
Kanpur with delays of sixteen months and submitted to Department of
Agriculture and Cooperation, Government of India in March 2010. Thus,
during 2007-10, funds were utilised without any District Agriculture Plans.

Though District Planning Committees were constituted (December 2007),
the work of preparation of District Agriculture Plans/State Agriculture Plan
was outsourced to Agriculture Finance Corporation, Lucknow and UP
Industrial Consultants Limited, Kanpur. These agencies did not follow the
envisaged bottom-up approach for planning

It is recommended that while preparing the District Agriculture Plans/State
Agriculture Plan, bottom up approach should be adopted to ensure
preparation of plans relevant to local area duly adhering to the timeline.

Financial Management

Against = 2,564.14 crore provided (2007-13) by Government of India,
Government of Uttar Pradesh utilised only हैं 2,493.28 crore for projects
under Stream I and Stream II. There were delays ranging between one to
eleven months in release of funds by Government of Uttar Pradesh to the
nodal department. There were instances of diversion of % 28.45 crore.

ee)


Audit Report (General an ‘ector) for the year ended 31 March 2013

It is recommended that funds may be released to the executing agencies
within the stipulated time and diversion of funds should be avoided.

Implementation of projects

Expenditure of = 104.09 crore was incurred on the project ‘Energisation of
Tube Wells’, which was neither included in District Agriculture Plans/State
Agriculture Plan nor was eligible for assistance under Rashtriya Krishi Vikas
Yojna. Similarly, हैं 21.60 crore was incurred on purchase of tractors which
was not admissible under the scheme.

State Level Sanctioning Committee (SLSC) approved (April 2010) the
project of हैं 46.41 crore for payment of incentives for certified seeds to
farmers and handling charges to UP Beej Vikas Nigam, though the project
was not included in District Agriculture Plans/State Agriculture Plan.

Rupees 21.89 crore was spent on implementation of Bringing Green
Revolution in Eastern India in National Food Security Mission districts,
though the scheme was to be implemented in Non-NFSM districts.

Expenditure of हैं 5.78 crore was incurred during 2009-10 for clearing the
liability created under Macro-mode of Agriculture scheme, despite the fact
that subsidy on distribution of seeds was not included in District Agriculture
Plans/State Agriculture Plan. Further, contrary to the opinion (August 2010)
of State Level Implementation Monitoring Committee, two projects of
₹ 6.08 crore and % 0.68 crore were approved (October 2010) by State Level
sanctioning Committee for payment of liability of 2009-10, pertaining to
Pradeshik Cooperative Federation and National Seed Corporation.

It is recommended that projects should be thoroughly vetted before
sanctioning the projects under the scheme.

Monitoring and Evaluation

Against the envisaged 21 meetings of SLSC during March 2008 to March
2013, only 14 meetings were held, of which holding of 10 meetings was
delayed by 10 to 134 days. Further, State Level Implementation Monitoring
Committee, constituted in April 2010, held only eight meetings upto
March 2013 against the required 32 monthly meetings.

It is recommended that monitoring and evaluation of the projects should be
strengthened for effective implementation.

Impact

The momentum of growth of Agriculture sector, 4.2 per cent during
2008-09, lost its way in 2009-10 to minus one per cent, after launching of
RKVY. It picked up during 2010-12 (4.2 per cent) but again declined to
2.60 per cent in 2012-13.

o—_—_—_—_————————


2.1.1 Introduction

With the objective to ensure annual growth rate of at least four per cent in
Agriculture sector, Government of India (Gol) launched (May 2007) Rashtriya
Krishi Vikas Yojna (RKVY), a centrally assisted (100 per cent) State Plan
scheme during the eleventh plan period. The main objectives of RKVY were:

e To incentivise States so as to increase public investment in agriculture and
allied sectors;

e To provide flexibility and autonomy to states in the process of planning
and executing agriculture and allied sector schemes;

e To ensure preparation of Agriculture Plans for districts and States, based
on agro-climatic conditions, availability of technology and natural
resources;

e To achieve the goal of reducing the yield gaps in important crops through
focused interventions;

e To maximise returns to the farmers in agriculture and allied sectors;

e To ensure that local needs/crops/priorities are better reflected in
agricultural plans of States; and

e To bring about quantifiable changes in production and productivity of
agriculture and allied sectors by addressing them in a holistic manner.

2.1.2 Organisational Structure

The Department of Agriculture and Cooperation (DAC), Ministry of
Agriculture, Government of India was responsible for budgetary controls,
release of funds and overall administration of the scheme.

At the State level, Principal Secretary, Agriculture Department was the nodal
head for according administrative and financial approvals. At the departmental
level, Director, Agriculture acted as State Level Nodal Agency’ (SLNA) for
RKVY and was assisted by an Additional Director, Agriculture for
coordinating with other departments and implementing agencies for
implementation and monitoring of the scheme.

A State Level Sanctioning Committee (SLSC) was constituted (November
2007) under the Chairmanship of Chief Secretary, Government of Uttar
Pradesh (GoUP) for sanctioning and monitoring the projects. A State Level
Implementation and Monitoring Committee (SLIMC) was also constituted
(April 2010) under the Chairmanship of Agriculture Production Commissioner
(APC), GoUP, for examination, monitoring and evaluation of the projects.

1 Though ‘State Agriculture Management and Extension Training Institute’ (SAMETI), Rehmankhera, Lucknow had
been nominated (November 2007) as State Level Nodal Agency for implementation of RKVY.

ee)


udit Report (General and ar ended 31 March 20

At district level, ‘Agriculture Technical Management Agency’ (ATMA), was
made (November 2007) responsible for implementation of the scheme. In
addition, Deputy Director, Agriculture was nominated (May 2010) as district
level nodal officer for monitoring the scheme activities of agriculture and
other line” departments. The organisational chart is given in Appendix-2.1.1.

2.1.3 Scope of Audit and Methodology

Funds under RKVY were made available to States in two distinct streams, viz,
Stream-I for specific projects and Stream-II for strengthening the existing
State sector schemes. With a view to ascertain adequacy in implementation of
the scheme, Audit selected 21 out of 263 projects of Stream-lI, 2 projects out of
101 projects of Stream-II and five projects of sub-schemes pertaining to
Agriculture and allied departments for 2007-13 by adopting the criteria for
selection of Sectors, projects and districts as detailed in Appendix-2.1.2 and
the performance audit was conducted’ in 29 districts* between May and
September 2013. Physical verification of projects and beneficiaries was also
conducted for arriving at audit conclusions.

An Entry Conference was held on 21 March 2013 with the Agriculture
Production Commissioner. During entry conference the audit objectives, audit
criteria, scope and methodology of audit were discussed and agreed upon. Exit
Conference was held with the Government on 5 May 2014 wherein the facts
and figures were admitted and the recommendations accepted. Replies of the
State Government were received and suitably incorporated in the report.

2.1.4 Audit Objectives
The Performance Audit of RKVY was taken up to assess whether:

e Planning process for the implementation of the scheme was effective and
according to the RKVY guidelines;

e Financial management ensured adequate and timely availability of funds
and their effective and economic utilisation;

e Projects were implemented according to the regulatory structure in place;
the intended objectives of the projects were achieved; and Nodal
Agency/Agriculture Department effectively coordinated with various
departments and implementing agencies;

e Internal control mechanism were put in place to ensure efficient and
effective monitoring and control] over implementation; and

7Animal Husbandry, Dairy development, Minor irrigation, Sericulture, Sugarcane Department and UP DASP ete.

Department; Managing Director, Pradeshik Cooperative Dairy Federation; Project Coordinator, UP Diversified

e The objectives of maximising returns to the farmers in agriculture and

The audit criteria have been derived from the following sources:

e Guidelines of RKVY issued (May 2007) by Department of Agricultural
and Cooperation, Ministry of Agriculture;

e Information collected from the website of RKVY (rkvy.nic.in);
e Reports of Planning Commission and other reports> of GoUP;

e District Agriculture Plans (DAPs)/Comprehensive State Agricultural
Plan (SAP) for 2007-12;

e Guidelines and Action Plans/Detailed Project Reports (DPRs) of
projects;

e Instructions/guidelines issued at State/District level; and

e General Financial Rules, Uttar Pradesh Budget Manual, Store Purchase
Rules and relevant Government Orders of GoUP.

Audit acknowledges the cooperation and assistance extended by the State
Government and their officials at various stages of Performance audit.

As per Paragraphs 3.1, 3.2 and 3.5 of Gol guidelines, the States were required
to prepare DAPs duly adopting bottom-up approach whereby a citizen survey
was to be conducted to ascertain local needs and the draft plan proposal of a
village was to be initiated by Gram Sabha and approved by Gram Panchayat
before preparation of DAPs and shelf of projects at district level. A
comprehensive SAP was to be prepared by integrating DAPs within three
months of issuance of guidelines. The Agriculture Department (Nodal
Agency) was to finalize SAP and submit the same to the State Planning
Department (SPD) for integrating it with the overall State Plans, to enable
SPD to submit the same to the Planning Commission of India as a part of its
State Plan exercise. We observed:

e Though District Planning Committees were constituted (December
2007) to determine policies, programmes and priorities for development
of each district alongwith preparation of development plans, the work
of preparation of DAPs and compilation of SAP for 2007-12

5 Five year plan and Annual Plans for the period 2007-12.

ep


udit Report (General and ar ended 31 March 20

was outsourced (2008-09) to Agriculture Finance Corporation (AFC), a
deemed Government of India company and Uttar Pradesh Industrial
Consultants Limited, Kanpur (UPICO) which delayed the submission of
report (March 2010) by 28 months. The bottom-up approach for
planning was not followed as the draft proposals were not prepared by
Gram Panchayats and compiled by next level of Panchayats
(block/district). Further, participatory groups°were not consulted.

e Citizen surveys were conducted only in five villages’ of each block in
each district without any criteria for selecting these villages*. Further,
agro-climatic conditions and available natural resources were not
surveyed’. Only constraints faced by the farmers were taken into
consideration and, therefore, DAPs did not depict the correct picture of
other facets related to agriculture and allied sectors”. It also did not
include financial requirement'’, sources of funding’ and vision
document’? for development of agriculture and allied sectors.

e Shelves of projects were also not prepared by any district. The nodal and
allied departments prepared projects, including some non-permissible'*
projects which were also sanctioned by SLSC.

Thus, during 2007-10, the scheme was implemented without any specific plan,
despite spending हैं 7.10 crore to prepare these DAPs.

The Government accepted (March 2014) the delayed preparation of
DAPs/SAP and stated that DAPs were prepared as per the guidelines issued
by Planning Commission of India, according to terms of reference of
Agriculture department and with the assistance of village, block and district
level agriculture planning units.

The reply was not acceptable as DAPs was prepared with a delay of 28
months without following the bottom up approach. Coverage of citizen survey
was not adequate, agro-climatic conditions and available natural resources
were not surveyed and assistance of village, block and district agriculture
planning units was taken only for gathering information.

resulted in non-implementation of various components of the projects as
discussed in paragraph 2.1.9.7.

The Government accepted (March 2014) the facts.

2.1.8.5 Inadmissible expenditure

As per paragraph 3.6 of RKVY guidelines, a State was permitted to use upto
one per cent of its total RK VY funds for administrative expenditure, which
included payments to consultants, recurring expenses of various kinds, staff
costs etc but there was no provision of charging the same on funds allocated
for projects under Stream-I and II.

(a) We observed that against the total allocation of RK VY of % 2,642.45 crore
during 2007-13, ₹ 25.35 crore”? (one per cent share) was allocated towards
administrative fund. Of the allocated fund, administrative expenditure of
= 7.07 crore was booked.

Although there was no provision of charging recurring expenses on funds
allocated for projects under Stream-I and II, implementing agencies
spent (March 2013) funds totaling हैं 22.10 crore (i) UP DASP spent
16.39 crore on making payments to NGOs, (ii) PCDF spent हैं 0.27 crore
(10 per cent) on Technical Supervision Fee (TSF) on purchase of equipment
for modernization and strengthening of processing capacity of NOIDA dairy
plant, (iii) Pradeshik Dairy Cooperative Federation spent ~ 3.76 crore as
overheads (iv) AD spent हैं 0.89 crore on hiring of manpower for Soil Testing
Laboratories and ¥ 0.79 crore on electricity charges from funds allocated for
projects under Stream-I instead of meeting it from one per cent allocated for
administrative expenditure.

2.1.8.6 Outstanding utilisation certificates

delays of five to 28 months. UCs of = 70.85 crore were outstanding at
the end of 2012-13 (Appendix-2.].4). Further, UCs of the entire amount
(® 1,849.15 crore), received during 2009-12, were sent by the State
Government to DAC without obtaining UCs of % 33.78 crore from the
implementing agencies.

e During 2011-12, % 38.28 crore was transferred to the districts for

e During 2009-13, advances of = 11.64 crore to UPCL, हैं 2.96 crore to

for ‘Strengthening of Government farms and seed production’. UC of the

AD without expenditure details/vouchers of the advances from UPCL,
UPSKNN and NEDA.

e During 2008-13, UC of % 253.70 crore was sent by Project Coordinator,
UP Diversified Agriculture Support Project (UPDASP) to the nodal

2.1.9 Implementation of Projects

2.1.9.1 Selection of beneficiaries

Project Guidelines laid down the procedure for selection of beneficiaries and
in some cases included male-female ratio and prescribed the percentage for
Scheduled Castes (SCs)/Scheduled Tribes (STs) beneficiaries also. Audit of
test-checked districts revealed:

e Under ‘Five days training and extension facilities to livestock owners’
selection of trainees was not approved by the committee constituted for
the purpose in 2370 out of 29 test-checked districts. Further, the selection
criteria of male and female trainees in the ratio of 50:50 was not ensured
in 13 training programmes conducted during 2010-11 in four districts*'
and 10 programmes conducted in four districts” (out of 29 test-checked
districts) during 2011-12.

e During 2009-12, mandatory approval of the committee of Swarnjayanti
Gram Swarojgar Yojna was not taken before finalizing the beneficiaries
in 14 districts’ under ‘Establishment of goat farming units’.

e Under ‘Sugarcane seed development programme’ norms for identifying
and selecting 21 per cent of the beneficiaries of SCs/STs by the
Sugarcane Development Council (SDC) were not adhered to in 20
districts.*4

e Under ‘Soil Health Improvement Programme’ (Dhaincha seed
production), against required selection as per norms of selecting the
beneficiaries in the open meetings of Gram Sabha, they were selected
arbitrarily on first come first serve basis.

e Under BGREI-Creation of Irrigation potential- the beneficiaries were
not selected, as required, by a Joint Committee of Agriculture and MI
Departments in any of the test-checked districts.

The Government accepted (March 2014) the facts.
2.1.9.2 Implementation of projects not eligible for funding under RKVY

Paragraph 7.1.1 of RKVY guidelines required the nodal agency to satisfy itself
that the project fulfilled the objectives of the scheme before recommending the
DPRs and produce an eligible list of projects for assistance. However, it was
noticed that four projects costing 365.88 crore were taken up for execution
during 2008-12, though these were not eligible for funding under RKVY.

2.1.9.4 Excess/Unfruitful expenditure
6) Special Dairy Development Project

To increase and incentivise milk production, SLSC sanctioned (July 2011)
‘Special Dairy Development Project’ under ‘National Mission for Protein
Supplement’ at = 18.35 crore. The project, inter alia, included distribution of
fodder to cattlemen. The fodder was to be supplied to the beneficiaries during
April to June (lean period) for milching animals.

We observed that हैं 2.27 crore and % 2.02 crore were released to Cattle Feed
Plant (CFP), Varanasi and Cattle Feed Factory (CFF), Meerut on 5 and 16
June 2012 respectively even though June was the last month of the lean period
for supply of fodder to district milk federations without specifying any time
schedule for start and completion of supply. At the time of release of funds,
the rate of fodder in CFP, Varanasi and CFF, Meerut was
₹ 10,200 per MT and ₹ 10,800 per MT respectively.

he units supplied (CFP, Varanasi: 935 MT and CFF, Meerut: 1,327

resulting in non-achievement of objectives of the scheme.

(iii) = Soil Health Improvement Programme

(a) Green manure component

Green manure component of the programme was executed during 2009-12
with the objective to improve soil health. Under the programme, free of cost
Dhaincha/Sanai seeds were to be provided to beneficiaries. The seeds were to
be sown in the areas covered under Wheat-Rice crop cycle and after 45 days
of its sowing, the crop was to be flipped and paddy crop was to be sown in the
flipped area. The programme was to be implemented during the months of
May and June and in the areas deficient in bio-carbon. It was, therefore,
necessary to supply and distribute the seeds to the farmers by 15 May.

> Audit observed that the supply order for one lakh quintals of seed for
2010-11 was issued to Beej Vikas Nigam (BVN) during the month of
implementation of programme (May 2010) and during 2009-10 and
2011-12, orders for supply of seed of 0.69 lakh quintals and 1.13 lakh
quintals respectively were issued in the month of March. Against the
total quantity of 2.79 lakh quintals of seed during 2009-12, 1.20 lakh
quintals of seed were supplied by BVN after sowing time. This resulted
in unjustified expenditure of € 61.43 crore and non-achievement of the
objective of scheme. Audit of test-checked districts revealed:

e Requirement of Dhaincha seeds was decided without obtaining
demand from districts,” ascertaining acreage of paddy coverage and
assessment of agro-climatic conditions®'. This resulted in allotment
of 11,514.85 quintals of seeds valuing ~ 4.42 crore to Banda,

was approved (October 2010) by SLSC. Action Plan of MMA scheme for

utilising RKVY funds. This resulted in unjustified expenditure of हैं 33.55
crore (% 14.36 crore and % 19.19 crore) on distribution of additional quantities
of wheat and rice seeds during 2010-11 and 2011-12 respectively.

In reply, the Government accepted (March 2014) utilization of RKVY funds for
MMA scheme. No replies were furnished to other findings.

Minor Irrigation Works (Deep Borings, Medium Deep Tube-wells,
Dr. B R Ambedkar tube-wells and Dr. Ambedkar Community Tube-wells)

Considering the worsening situation of ground water, SAP recommended
(January 2009) to increase the share of surface water for irrigation, such as
canals and reduce dependence on ground water to restrict its depletion. As per
Para 6.3 of Gol guidelines, SLSC was required to approve projects consistent
with the State Agricultural Plans.

In 10 test-checked districts,” in contravention of recommendations of SAP,
subsidy of हैं 9.12 crore was utilised in Minor Irrigation Works (224 Deep
borings, 564 medium deep tube-wells and 48 Dr. B.R. Ambedkar tube-wells)
during 2010-12 in blocks categorised as over- exploited and critical in surveys
conducted (2009) by Ground Water Board, as detailed in Appendix-2.1.6.

In reply, the Government stated (March 2014) that categorisation of dark zone
blocks was published only in May 2012. The reply was not acceptable as SAP
had recommended in January 2009 to reduce dependence on ground water to
restrict its depletion and Ground Water Board had categorised the over-
exploited and critical blocks in March 2009.

2.1.9.7 Implementation of projects after expiry of cropping season

Accelerated Fodder Development Programme (AFDP) was implemented
(2011-12) by DAC with the objective™ to accelerate production and to ensure
the availability of fodder throughout the year.

DAC released (August and December 2011) ~ 30 crore for implementation
during Zayed” 2011-12. As per provisions, fodder production kits were to be
made available to the targeted beneficiaries by 31 January 2012 and sowing of
seeds was to be ensured by beneficiaries during February and March 2012.

We observed that:

e Action Plan” of the project was released belatedly (December 2011) by
AD and partial financial sanctions of = 15 crore were issued in March
2012. Accordingly, supply orders were placed in January 2012 on BVN
for procurement of 32,380 quintals”” of seeds. The delivery of seeds was
made during March to June 2012, after sowing season was over.

e Due to non-maintenance of records of sowing of seeds in the
test-checked districts, actual utilisation of seeds by the farmers was not
ascertained.

e AD did not provide other recommended inputs” in fodder production
kits and post-harvest management component was also not executed
during Zayed 2012. Further, = 15.92 crore” was spent on distribution of
seeds against the normative cost of = 7.20 crore (र 1000 per hectare) for
coverage of 72,000 hectares of land.

Thus, delayed supply of seeds and non-distribution of other inputs and post-
harvest implements during Zayed 2012, resulted in avoidable expenditure of
% 8.72 crore, besides non-fulfilment of the objectives.

No comments were offered by the Government (March 2014).

e 16 project was implemented for three years upto 2011-12. Against the

e Against the prescribed norm for purchase of 20 does for establishment of
each goat farming unit, only two, five and ten does were purchased by

explanation has been called from the officials of Jalaun, Mainpuri and
Rae bareli.

respectively were established during 2010-11, against the target of 10
units for each district. Similarly, in Baghpat and Kashiram Nagar only
three and nine units were established respectively during 2011-12,
against the target of 10 units for each district.

Thus, the objective of generating adequate employment was only partially
achieved, as the target for establishing 10 goat farming units every year in
every district was not fulfilled during 2010-12. The CVOs of the five
test-checked districts stated in their reply that due to non-availability of
beneficiaries the targets were not achieved.

The Government accepted (March 2014) the facts and stated that all the
targeted units were established in Bahraich and Kashiram Nagar. The reply
was not acceptable as CVOs of the concerned districts stated that targets were
not achieved.

Special Dairy Development Project

With the objective to increase and incentivise milk production and holistic
development of dairy sector, SLSC sanctioned (July 2011) ‘Special Dairy
Development Project’ under ‘National Mission for Protein Supplement’ at
र₹ 18.35 crore. The project, inter alia, provided subsidy and loan through
banks for establishment of dairy units which was to be provided to targeted
beneficiaries. Audit observed:

e Against the target for establishment of 105 mini dairies of 25 animals
each, first installment of bank loan was released only to 49 targeted
beneficiaries till March 2013. Further, out of these 49 targeted
beneficiaries, second installment was released to only 12 units. Even the
units, which received second installment, did not establish complete
units of 25 animals. As a result, the objective of the project to increase
milk production and develop dairy sector was only partially achieved.

e Against the target for establishment of 50 heifer rearing units,
82 beneficiaries were selected but loan applications of only 70 targeted
beneficiaries were forwarded to rural banks. Loans were, however,
sanctioned to only two targeted beneficiaries. Against 39 beneficiaries,
only 22 targeted beneficiaries received subsidy and first installment of
bank loan and two targeted beneficiaries purchased 25 heifers. Thus,
only four per cent of the targeted beneficiaries established complete
units resulting in non-achievement of the objectives of the scheme.

The Government accepted (March 2014) the observation and stated that the
project is linked with bank loans and the bank took time in appraisal and
sanctioning of loan applications.

Effective implementation of RKVY required coordinated functioning of
different agencies/departments involved in implementation of RKVY. We
observed lack of coordination between State and district level implementing
agencies and different committees involved in implementation and monitoring
of the scheme, as discussed below:

2.1.10 Monitoring and Evaluation

The progress and implementation of the scheme was monitored by SLSC,
SLIMC and nodal department/implementing agencies at state level and nodal
officer at district level.

2.1.10.1 Inadequate monitoring by SLSC

(a) As per Paragraphs 6.4 and 6.5 of RKVY guidelines, GoUP was
required to notify the constitution of SLSC within a month of publication of
the guidelines. SLSC was to meet as often as required but at least once a
quarter.

RKVY guidelines were issued in August 2007 and first SLSC meeting was
held in March 2008. We observed:

e Against the requirement of 21, only 14 meetings were held between 3
March 2008 and 31 March 2013.

e 14 meetings were delayed by 10 to 134 days.

e 18 projects, though not included in the agenda, were sanctioned by SLSC
during 2008-13.

(b) Timely completion of projects is necessary for effective
implementation of the scheme and extension of benefits to the targeted
beneficiaries. Accordingly, RKVY guidelines made SLSC responsible for
monitoring the progress of the sanctioned projects/schemes.

Audit of test-checked projects revealed that 10 works relating to five projects
valuing हैं 18.78 crore were incomplete (September 2013) for periods ranging
from two to four years beyond completion schedule (Appendix-2.1.9), as
illustrated below:

” Mainly included purchase of combine harvester, tractor, mobile processing plant ete.

e The works of construction of Silk Exchange, Varanasi (% 2.26 crore),

and Fencing work of Government Silk Farm, Bhainsakasada
(® 0.05 crore), Mirzapur were awarded to PACCFED. Similarly, the

to UPCL. These works were incomplete even after lapse of periods
ranging between 30 and 39 months from their scheduled dates of
completions due to delays on the part of construction agency
(₹ 7.90 crore).

e Tube-wells constructed (% 7.23 crore) under Minor Irrigation Works
(Deep boring, medium deep tube-wells, Dr. B.R. Ambedkar tube-wells

e The projects ‘Strengthening and renovation of communication

completed even after spending % 0.31 crore and delays ranging between
12 and 25 months.

The above instances indicate inadequate monitoring of projects by SLSC.

The Government accepted (March 2014) the facts and stated that steps would
be taken to make SLSC meetings more effective in future.

2.1.10.2 Inadequate monitoring by SLIMC
As per paragraph 8.1 of revised guidelines issued (July 2008) by Gol, SLIMC,
was to be constituted, meet every month and submit its report to SLSC.

Audit observed that the constitution of SLIMC was approved by SLSC in
April 2010. The first SLIMC meeting was held in August 2010. Only eight
SLIMC meetings were held between August 2010 and March 2013 against the
required 32 meetings. Further, though social audit was proposed (August
2010) by SLIMC but SLSC issued no directives (September 2013).

The Government accepted (March 2014) the facts.
2.1.10.3 Inadequate monitoring by nodal department
Paragraph 5.2 of RKVY guidelines provided for monitoring of the projects by

nodal department. Guidelines of various projects also envisaged monitoring
of implementation of the projects by nodal department/implementing agencies.

nodal officers. Further, there was no document showing monitoring of the

2.1.10.4 Evaluation of the projects

As per RKVY guidelines, SLSC was required to carry out evaluation studies
from time to time. We observed:

e Against 254 projects implemented during 2007-12, the SLSC awarded
third party evaluation of 56" , 477 and 457 projects (total 148 projects)
to Planning Research and Action Institute (PRAI), Rajya Niyojan
Sansthan, Lucknow, Giri Institute of Development Studies, Lucknow
and UP Council of Agriculture Research, Lucknow (UPCAR) of which
only six, 37 and 37 projects (total 80) were evaluated by these institutes
upto March 2013.

e The evaluation of two test-checked projects conducted by these institutes
indicated shortcomings in their implementation as discussed below:

(i) Evaluation of ‘Saghan Mini Dairy Pariyojna (SMDP)’, conducted
(December 2011) by Giri Institute of Development Studies revealed
difficulties like shortage of funds for construction of shades, complex
bank formalities, higher rate of interest on bank loan, sanction of
insufficient amount for purchase of animals etc.

(ii) Evaluation of Agrimart at Lucknow and Hapur conducted (January
2012) by Giri Institute of Development Studies, Lucknow pointed out
improper selection of site.

(iii) Evaluation of the Sugarcane seed programme, conducted (June 2012) by
Indian Institute of Sugarcane Research, Lucknow, in Lakhimpur Kheri
and. Bareilly’, indicated distribution of micronutrients without soil
analysis, requirement of subsidization of some more implements, need of
maintenance support system for the implements etc.

The Government accepted (March 2014) the findings of evaluation studies and
stated that third party evaluation of projects by nominated agencies were in
progress.

2.1.11 Impact Evaluation

Uttar Pradesh has a population of 19.96 crore (as per Census, 2011). Nearly 65
per cent of its population is dependent on agriculture, directly or indirectly. It
produces 19.74 per cent of the total food grains of the country. During
2007-13, centrally planned/sponsored schemes like NFSM, MMA, ATMA,
ISOPAM etc. and State Plan Schemes like Schemes for seed, manure and
fertilizers, plant protection, commercial crops, soil conservation etc. were
implemented alongwith RKVY.

Year-wise production of food grains, oilseeds, contribution of agriculture
sector to GSDP and growth rate is given in Table 3:

Audit observed that:

(i) The average productivity of Cereals, Pulses and Oilseeds in the State
was 27.74 quintals per hectare, 8.63 quintals per hectare and
8.37 quintals per hectare respectively during 2011-12. The production of
food grain and oilseeds increased significantly during 2007-13.

(ii) Soil health had deteriorated during the last few years due to
indiscriminate and imbalanced use of chemical fertilizers. The
consumption of chemical fertilizers during 2012-13 was 92,62,000 MT

™ Website address: www.rkvy.nic.in,

ee)


Audit Report (General r) for the year ended 31 March 2013

against cultivable areas of 1.66 crore hectares, whereas during 2007-08 it
was only 59,92,000 MT against the cultivable areas of 1.64 crore
hectares. Further, the recommended fertilizer use in terms of Nitrogen,
Phosphorus and Potassium (NPK) ratio in the State was 4:2:1, whereas
the present NPK usage ratio was 13:5:1. Due to imbalanced use of
chemical fertilizer, status of soil health did not improve even after
implementation of Soil health improvement programme under RKVY.

(iii) The contribution of Agriculture sector to Gross State Domestic Product
gradually decreased from 27.10 to 22.70 per cent during 2007-13.
Further, the growth rate of Agriculture sector was (-) one per cent and.
2.6 per cent during 2009-10 and 2012-13 respectively, which were less
than the targeted growth rate of 4 per cent.

The Government accepted (March 2014) the facts.
2.1.12 Conclusion

Bottom-up approach of RKVY for the planning process, right from grass root
to the State level was largely not adopted. Preparation of DAPs/SAP were
delayed which led to implementation of projects without specific plan during
2007-10. Improper planning and non-vetting of DPRs, led to instances 0
implementation of inadmissible projects and overlapping of projects with the
existing schemes. Funds under Stream-I and II remained under-utilize
whereas funds of Stream- II were diverted to Stream-I during 2010-11 and
2012-13. Project funds were used for administrative expenditure whereas
funds allocated under administrative expenditure remained unutilised. Funds
were diverted for purposes other than those approved by SLSC. Instances o:
excess, unjustified and unfruitful expenditure were noticed.

SLSC was constituted after six months of issuance of guidelines and its
meetings were not held at prescribed intervals. SLIMC was also constituted
after more than two years of issuance of guidelines. There were instances o:
lack of coordination between SLSC and SLIMC. Monitoring mechanism did
not work well and evaluation of the projects was not always carried out. The
targeted growth rate of four per cent of agriculture sector every year was not
achieved during 2008-13.

2.1.13 Recommendations

Bottom-up approach for planning should be adopted to ensure
preparation of plans relevant to local area and only projects permissible
under RKVY and consistent with DAPs/SAP should be taken up.

The projects should be properly vetted before sanctioning by SLSC after
due examination.

Funds may be released to the executing agencies within the stipulated
time and diversion of funds should be avoided.

The projects should be framed in such a manner so as to avoid
overlapping with existing schemes.

Monitoring and Evaluation of the projects should be strengthened.

Rural Development Department

2.2... Performance Audit of Implementation of Indira Awaas Yojana
Executive Summary

Performance Audit of implementation of Indira Awaas Yojana (IAY), the
tural housing programme, was conducted during April to August 2013
covering the period from 2008-09 to 2012-13. A sum of % 6,622.18 crore
was spent in the State on the implementation of IAY during 2008-13.

Audit revealed many deficiencies in both financial management and
Programme implementation:

e The test-checked districts did not maintain the database for housing
shortage. Consequently targets distributed to the blocks were not as per
the criteria fixed in IAY guidelines, denying more funds to the blocks
with larger number of houseless people.

State Government should ensure maintenance of proper database for
the housing shortage.

e 211 beneficiaries of Khajua Block of Fatehpur were not found in
Below Poverty Line lists and 19,131 beneficiaries in four districts were
not found in IAY waitlists. Further, 428 beneficiaries in 5 districts were
not found in either of the lists.

The Government should ensure identification of beneficiaries only from
BPL/IAY waitlists.

e As against sanction of one house on one Below Poverty Line (BPL)
number, three districts sanctioned 116 houses against 57 BPL numbers.
Further, 22 ineligible beneficiaries already having pucca houses were
sanctioned house under IAY.

The Government should ensure sanction of house to eligible
beneficiaries.

e In2012-13, Gol reduced 80 per cent the targets of houses fixed by it for
6 test-checked District Rural Development Agencies (DRDA). Even
then achievement against the revised targets in 12 districts was only
56 per cent.

Full achievement of the annual targets should be ensured by the
Government.

e In six test-checked districts, 10 to 64 per cent houses were sanctioned
in the name of male members instead of female members of the family.
Result of beneficiary survey revealed that 974 houses were sanctioned
to male beneficiaries though 930 of these households had female
members.

O———— -नन् न्न्नन-नीनकान


Chapter formance Audit

The Government should ensure sanction of houses in the name of
female members of the family.

e 5196 shares were not released timely and the delay ranged between two
and 344 days in test-checked districts.

The Government should ensure timely release of State share.

e 11 2012-13, due to higher opening balances in two DRDAs and short
release of State share in three DRDAs, Gol imposed financial cuts to
the extent of = 9.41 crore.

The Government should ensure that the opening balance is within the
prescribed limit.

e JAY fund of हैं 72.06 crore was diverted for sanction of houses in
villages under Ambedkar and Lohia Gram Yojanas of the State
Government.

Diversion of funds should be avoided.

e Four districts parked IAY funds of हैं 15.39 crore during (2009-12)
because eligible Scheduled Caste and Scheduled Tribe beneficiaries
were not available and three districts kept IAY funds of % 55.68 crore
in Personal Ledger Accounts opened in Government treasuries.

The Government should ensure utilisation of funds by correctly
identifying beneficiaries and avoid keeping of funds in PLAs.

e Due to inadequate IEC (Information, Education and Communication)
activities in the State, the beneficiaries were not aware of the facilities
to be provided under convergence.

IEC activities should be vigorously carried out by the state
government.

e  Grievances/Complaints redressal and public vigilance mechanism were
not put in place by the State Government. Social Audits were also not
undertaken.

The Government should put in place grievance redressal mechanism
and system for Social Audit.

2.2.1 Introduction

Indira Awaas Yojana (IAY), a public housing scheme for the houseless poor
families and those living in kutchcha houses with a component for providing
house sites to the landless poor was made an independent scheme as a part of
larger strategy of rural poverty eradication with effect from 1 January 1996.
The objective of IAY was primarily to help in construction/upgradation of
dwelling units of rural Below Poverty Line (BPL) householders belonging to
members of Schedule Caste/Schedule Tribe (SC/ST), freed bonded labourers,
minorities and other non-SC/STs by providing them a lump sum financial
assistance.

ee)


Audit Report (Gener:

JAY is designed to enable BPL households, identified by the community
through Gram Sabhas following the criteria suggested for such identification
from time to time, to build their houses or get house sites with financial and
technical assistance from the Government.

As per IAY guidelines, at least 60 per cent of the fund was to be spent for
SC/ST BPL households. A maximum of 40 per cent of the fund was to be
spent for non-SC/ST BPL rural households. Fifteen per cent of IAY funds in
the State were for the beneficiaries from among the minorities and three per
cent of the above categories were for physically and mentally challenged.

TAY is a centrally sponsored scheme shared between Government of India
(Gol) and State Government in the ratio of 75:25 respectively.

2.2.1.1 Organisational Structure

Rural Development Department (RDD), headed by the Principal Secretary, is
the nodal department for administration and implementation of the programme
at the Government level and Commissioner at Commissionerate level.
Schematic representation of organisational structure at the State, District,
Block and Gram Panchayat level is given below:

State Level

Principal Secretary, Rural Development
(Overall administration and monitoring of the scheme)

Commissioner, Rural Development Department
(Overall implementation of the scheme)

Deputy Commissioner, [AY
(Day-wise implementation of the
scheme)

Additional Commissioner, Finance
and Accounts
(Release of State share)

District level

Chief Development Officer

(Physical and financial sanction of houses)

rict Rural Development Agency
(Implementation at District level)

Block Development Officer
(Implementation at Block level)

Gram Panchayat

Gram Panchayat Secretary/Gram Vikas Adhikari
(Implementation at Village level)

(Source: Information provided by Rural Development Department)

formance Audit

2.2.1.2 Audit Objectives

The performance audit was undertaken to ascertain whether:

The systems and procedures in place for identification and selection of the
target groups and the processes for allotment, construction and
upgradation of dwelling units were adequate and conformed to the
provisions of the scheme;

e The physical performance under IAY in terms of number of units
constructed and upgraded was as planned and targetted and that the
constructions adhered to the quality and financial parameters set out in
the scheme guidelines;

e = The allocation and release of funds under IAY were made in an adequate
and timely manner and that these were utilised economically and
efficiently in accordance with provisions of the scheme;

e The convergence of IAY activities with other programmes as envisaged,
was effectively achieved and ensured availability of a completely
functional dwelling unit; and

e The mechanism in place for monitoring and evaluation of the outcomes of
the programme were adequate and effective.

2.2.1.3 Audit criteria and methodology
The audit criteria have been derived from the following sources:

e Guidelines of JAY issued by the Ministry of Rural Development (MoRD),
Government of India;

e —Circulars/instructions issued by MoRD and the State Government;

e Periodical reports/ returns prescribed by the State Government; and

e Joint physical verification of houses and beneficiary survey! carried out
by Audit with Gram Panchayat Secretaries/Gram Vikas Adhikaries, the
village level functionary.

In beneficiary survey, a set of questions were asked from the beneficiaries and documents prepared for their replies
were signed both by Audit and the beneficiaries.

eee हु)


dit Report (Gener:

The performance audit commenced with an entry conference with RDD on
4 April 2013 wherein the audit methodology, scope, objectives and criteria
were discussed. The draft report was issued to the State Government on
23 September 2013 and the responses received have been suitably
incorporated. An exit conference was held with State Government (Principal
Secretary, RD) on 27 January 2014 wherein the audit findings and
recommendations were discussed.

The performance audit of IAY for the period 2008-13 was conducted during
April to August 2013 in the office of the Principal Secretary, Rural
Development, Government of Uttar Pradesh (GoUP); Commissioner, Rural
Development; 15 sampled districts out of 71; 35 out of 160 Blocks within the
sampled districts; 350 out of 2,526 Gram Panchayats (GPs) in the sampled
Blocks; and 501 out of 585 Villages in sampled GPs including 3,197 out of
10,911 beneficiaries in sampled Villages (Appendix-2.2.1).

While sampling, the State was divided into four stratums” and the districts
were selected using PPSWR* method of statistical sampling techniques;
Blocks, Gram Panchayats (GP), Villages and beneficiaries were selected
using SRSWOR’ method.

Audit acknowledges the cooperation and assistance extended by the State
Government and their officials at various stages of the Performance audit.

For effective implementation of the scheme, systems and procedures are put in
place for identification and selection of the targetted beneficiaries for
construction and up-gradation of dwelling units. It includes allocation of
resources at various levels, planning at State and district levels, preparation of
a permanent IAY wait list and fixing of annual targets for construction of
houses.

Central assistance under 15 ४ was to be allocated to the State giving weightage
(75 per cent) to the shortage of rural housing and poverty ratio (25 per cent) as
per census data. Similarly, inter-district allocation of physical targets within a
State was to be made by giving weightage to housing shortage (75 per cent)
and rural SC/ST population (25 per cent) of the district. The targets for the

? Poorvanchal, West Zone, Awadh and Bundelkhand Zone.
3 PPSWR- Probability proportional to size with replacement.
* SRSWOR- Simple random sampling without replacement.

blocks within a district and Gram Panchayats within the Blocks were also to
be decided on the same principle. In order to allocate the targets vis-a-vis
funds and for minorities and physically and mentally challenged persons, it
was essential for the DRDAs to maintain block-wise database of housing
shortages of all these categories.

Further, clause 4.2 b (viii) of the guidelines, stipulates preparation of an
annual plan* by DRDAs.

e Test-check of districts revealed that the data of rural housing shortages
was cither not maintained® or was not reliable’. In the absence of reliable
data of housing shortages, the correctness of the allocations made to the
Blocks could not be verified in audit.

e After fixing the targets by MoRD, the DRDAs distributed the targets
amongst the blocks. The distribution of targets was based on population of
the district as against shortage of houses. Consequently, the Blocks having
higher shortages of houses were not provided more funds, as envisaged in
the guidelines. Some DRDAs (Gonda and Mainpuri) distributed the targets
up to GP level also but in Mainpuri it was not in accordance with IAY
guidelines. In Budaun, Fatehpur, Kushinagar and Lucknow the targets
were distributed to Blocks on the basis of a formula prescribed by the State
Government in 2003, which was not as per JAY guidelines.

e DRDAs of 15 test-checked districts did not prepare annual plans during
2008-13.

The Government did not furnish specific replies about non- preparation of
database, However, it stated (January 2014) that the plan for distribution of
the targets was made by DRDAs. The fact remains that due to non-preparation
of database and non-preparation of plans by DRDAs, the targetted
beneficiaries were not benefitted as intended.

2.2.2.2 Deficiencies in BPL and wait lists

Districts were to upload BPL lists prepared as per BPL Census 2002 on the
website of the district. From this list, an IAY permanent waitlist (in order of
seniority) was to be prepared and uploaded on the website. A separate list of
SC/ST families (in order of seniority) was also to be prepared from IAY
permanent waitlist to ensure sanction of required percentage of houses to
them.

We observed that BPL lists, though uploaded on the website of the
test-checked districts, had deficiencies viz., father’s name of BPL holder was

5 This plan was to include annual Block-wise/Panchayat-wise targets for construction of IAY houses and targets for

works.

© Amroha, Budaun, Fatehpur, Jhansi, Kushinagar and Lucknow had no data of housing shortages.

not indicated which led to incorrect identification of the beneficiaries while
sanctioning houses. Names of the same BPL number holders in the lists were
found to be repeated in Gonda. Budaun considered serial number of BPL list
as BPL number. Amroha, Deoria, Fatehpur, Gonda, Hamirpur, Lucknow,
Mathura, Maharajganj and Varanasi took family identification number as BPL
number and Jhansi, Kushinagar and Rampur took both the serial number as
BPL number along with the family identification number. Further beneficiary
survey revealed that 1,072 beneficiaries were not allotted houses as per
priority in the waitlist.

Thus, the housing shortage has been identified with the incorrect data.

The Government accepted (January 2014) the observation and stated that
father’s name will be incorporated while preparing new BPL lists. The
repetition of the name was because of errors while data feeding in computer.
The fact remains that the identification of targetted beneficiaries was
ineffective as the data available was incorrect.

2.2.2.3. Non-adoption of cluster approach

DRDAs, during a particular financial year, on the basis of allocations made
and targets fixed, were to decide the number of houses to be
constructed/upgraded, Panchayat wise cluster approach,* to facilitate better
supervision, convergence of schemes and economy in purchases.

We observed that in none of the test-checked districts, the villages were
divided into groups. The cluster approach was not adopted, denying the
intended benefit of better supervision and better facilities arising out of
convergence of schemes.

The Government stated (January 2014) that the houses were constructed by
beneficiaries on their own land and hence cluster approach was not possible.
The reply confirms that the cluster approach was not followed thereby denying
the intended benefits to the beneficiaries.

2.2.2.4 Selection of beneficiaries by Gram Sabha

The GPs were to draw a list of shelter-less families from the BPL list, strictly
in the order of ranking in the list. A separate list of SC/ST families was to be
derived from the larger IAY list to ensure that sanction of 60 per cent of
houses to SC/STs under the scheme. Once the lists were prepared, they were
required to be approved by the Gram Sabha, attended by a Government
servant as a nominee of the Collector.

During audit, the test-checked districts did not show the documents
confirming the approval of the Gram Sabha of the lists prepared for the
sanction of houses. However, in some districts (Rampur, Mainpuri and
Hamirpur), the approval of the lists in the meetings of the Gram Panchayat

* For this purpose, all the villages in a District/Block were to be divided into three groups and each group of villages
was to be provided funds every year

Oo


Chapter formance Audit

were observed. Further, the houses were sanctioned to the eligible families but
without mentioning the required priority.

No reply specific to the audit observation was furnished.
2.2.2.5 Irregular selection of beneficiaries

JAY guidelines provide for selection of beneficiaries according to their
eligibility. Audit scrutiny revealed that:

e Ten GPs of Khajuha Block of Fatehpur did not have BPL list of 2002.
Consequently, the BPL status and seniority of 211 beneficiaries sanctioned
(2008-13) houses could not be verified in audit.

e In Amroha, Deoria, Hamirpur and Maharajganj, the names of the selected
19,131 beneficiaries were not found in IAY waitlists and hence the
sanction of beneficiaries/houses, as per seniority, could not be confirmed
in audit.

e Amroha, Budaun, Deoria, Maharajganj and Rampur sanctioned houses to
428 beneficiaries, whose names were neither in BPL nor in IAY waitlists.

Thus, the selection of the beneficiaries was made outside the BPL and IAY
waitlists which was not admissible as per LAY guidelines.

The Government, while accepting the observations, assured (January 2014),
to take suitable action.

2.2.2.6 Sanction of houses to ineligible beneficiaries

Beneficiaries already having pucca houses were ineligible for a house under
JAY. Further, only one house was admissible against one BPL number.

Scrutiny revealed that:

e twenty two selected beneficiaries in Amroha and Deoria, who already had
pucca houses were sanctioned houses on grounds of being shelter less BPL
family members. Beneficiary survey also revealed that nine beneficiaries,
already having pucca houses, were sanctioned IAY house; and

e one hundred and sixteen houses were sanctioned to the beneficiaries
against 57 BPL identification numbers in Fatehpur, Mahrajganj and
Varanasi.

Thus, sanction was made more than once to the same family identification
number which was inadmissible.

The Government accepted (January 2014) the facts and stated that suitable
action would be taken. The fact remains that more than one house was
sanctioned to the same BPL family which was not admissible.

eee.)

2.2.3.1 Delayed release of State share

State share was to be released to DRDAs within one month of release of
Central assistance and a copy of the same was to be endorsed to MoRD. We
noticed in audit that the release of State share was delayed by two to 344 days
in the test-checked districts. Details are given in Appendix-2.2.8. The delay in
release of State share violated the provisions of LAY guidelines thereby
leading to delays in providing houses to the targeted beneficiaries.

2.2.3.2 Curtailment of financial assistance

(a) As per paragraph no. 4.2 b-ii of guidelines of IAY, the opening balance of
the districts should not exceed 10 per cent of the funds available during the
previous year. In case, the opening balance exceeds this limit, the Central
share was to be deducted proportionately (the excess of 10 per cent) at the
time of release of the second installment.

It was noticed that in Maharajganj (9.83%) and Rampur (13.42%), the opening
balances of 2012-13 exceeded the prescribed limit and consequently Gol
imposed cuts to the extent of = 6.18 crore and & 1.40 crore respectively.

Further, though Budaun, Deoria, Hamirpur, Jhansi, and Mathura had opening
balances of more (X 21.45 crore) than the prescribed limit during 2008-13, the
amount हैं 64.35 crore, due for deduction, was released by Gol while releasing
the second installments to the districts.

The Government stated that higher opening balance in Maharajganj was due
to inclusion of amount of un-cashed cheques in the closing balance of 2011-12
by Gol. In Rampur the higher balances were because of non-availability of
targetted SC/ST beneficiaries. The reply confirms that the targets fixed were
not based on realistic data base and thus, the observation is reiterated. This
further lead to non-utilisation of funds and revision of targets as discussed in
para 2.2.2.8.

“>


dit Report (General y ended 31 March 2013

(b) The State Government should make sufficient provision in its Budget
and all its contributions (including that of the previous years) due should be
released before the submission of the application for the second installment. In
the event of shortfall in State share, corresponding amount of Central share
(i.e. three times of the shortfall of State share) was deductible from the second
installment of the Central share of the current year.

We, in audit, noticed that हैं 96.50 lakh of State share was short released to
three districts in 2009-11, which led to Gol imposing cuts of हैं 1.83 crore in
Budaun, Kushinagar and Mainpuri.

In reply, the Government accepted (January 2014) the facts.
2.2.3.3 Diversion of fund

As per paragraph number 2.1 of the guidelines, sanction of houses in a village
was to be done only to the extent of eligible beneficiaries as per seniority fixed.
in IAY waitlist vis-a-vis target available for the village.

We, during audit of eight out of test-checked 15 DRDAs, noticed that the State
Government issued orders (July 2008 and August 2012) for providing houses
up to the level of saturation of villages under Ambedkar and Lohia Gram
Yojanas identified by it. The concerned DRDAs set apart the required number
of houses for these villages out of the total target received from Gol and only
the balance targets were distributed amongst remaining Blocks/Villages. In
test-check, 17,752 houses (® 72.06 crore!!) in eight districts were sanctioned
to the BPL families (SC/STs and others) of Lohiya and Ambedkar villages.
The identified villages belonged to the flagship schemes of the State
Government but were funded from IAY funds. District- wise details of houses
are given in Appendix-2.2.9. Thus the State Government financed its own
schemes from Gol funds.

2.2.3.4 Parking of funds

As per paragraph no.1.5 of the guidelines, parking of funds in Bank Accounts
should ordinarily be avoided. In case, a particular category of beneficiaries
was exhausted in a district, the funds should be utilised for sanctioning houses
to eligible beneficiaries of another category after getting due certificate from
the concerned DRDA.

We noticed in audit that four out of 15 test-checked districts parked’? ₹ 15.39
crore during 2009-12 stating non-availability of targetted beneficiaries of the
SC/ST category in the districts as the reason as discussed in para 2.2.2.8.

The Government, while confirming the facts, stated (January 2014) that the
funds were parked due to non-availability of SC/ST beneficiaries. The reply

We noticed in audit that:

e monthly bank reconciliation of balances was not done by any of the 15

2013. Possibility of misappropriation of funds could not be ruled out.

© beneficiary survey conducted on these beneficiaries disclosed that the

2.2.3.7 Payment of assistance on non-staggered basis

As per clause 4.10 of IAY guidelines, payments to beneficiaries were to be
made on staggered basis, ideally in two installments, the first with the sanction
order and the second when construction of house reaches the lintel level and
after receipt of a certificate to this effect from the concerned BDO.

We observed that:

e 251 houses were sanctioned in Kushinagar on 30.3.2010. While
sanctioning, full payments (both the amounts against first and second
installments) were released stating that the concerned BDO would watch
the release of funds by the bank in the beneficiaries account in two
installments;

e in 2009-10 in Kushinagar, the entire assistance (% 3.34 crore) to 955
beneficiaries was paid in one installment without ensuring the construction
of houses up to the lintel level;

e the entire assistance of हैं 11.20 lakh to 36 beneficiaries in Deoria,
& 31.95 lakh to 71 beneficiaries in Maharajganj, € 72.80 lakh to 208
beneficiaries in Mainpuri and % 1.35 lakh to 3 beneficiaries in Rampur
were paid in single installment.

e in six test-checked districts (Amroha, Deoria, Gonda, Hamirpur, Lucknow
and Varanasi) the payments to the beneficiaries were made in three
installments’ as against the prescribed two.

With the release of the installments, the DRDAs showed the houses as
complete in the progress reports. This was violative of IAY guidelines as the
completion of houses was not monitored by the DRDAs/BDOs concerned.

The Government, while accepting the observations, stated (January 2014) that
necessary directions to the DRDAs have been issued to examine the cases.

2.2.3.8 Payment of assistance without ensuring bank accounts

Funds under IAY were to be transferred directly into beneficiaries’ bank
accounts. For this purpose, as soon as beneficiaries were selected, they were to
be asked to open a Bank/Post Office account, in case they did not already have
one, and to intimate the same to GP/BDO/DRDA, as the case may be.

We noticed in audit that cheques (₹ 1.57 lakh) were issued in Amroha in
2012-13 without the details of bank account numbers of six beneficiaries.
