2.2.3.9 Non-release of second installments to the beneficiaries

Construction of IAY houses had to be completed within two years. Second
installments were to be released only after completion of the house up to lintel
level. In 2009-13, the second installments to 9,779 beneficiaries in Kushinagar
and 21 beneficiaries in Mainpuri and cight beneficiaries in 2009-10 in
Varanasi were not released for want of demands from the concerned blocks.
Further, in Maharajganj (Lakshmipur Block), the second installment to
88 beneficiaries was not released since 2011-12.

We observed that the districts showed their targets as achieved with the issue
of the first installments itself, however, physical completion of houses could
not be ascertained in audit.

The Government, while accepting the audit findings, stated (January 2014)
that the suitable actions are being taken by the districts. The fact remains that
non-release of second installment defeated the objective of providing houses to
the houseless.

2.2.3.10 Release of second installment without verification report from
BDOs

The State Government made provision for payment of second installment only
after completion of construction of house up to the lintel level.

In Budaun, first installments were released (2011-12) to 324 beneficiaries of
% 2,500 each only and subsequently (March 2012) the remaining amount of
= 42,500 was released (₹ 1.38 crore) without verification of construction of
house up to lintel level as with only % 2,500, as the first installment,
construction up to lintel level was not possible. The district issued cheque
amounting to हैं 1.20 crore again on 30 March 2013 for payment of the second
installment to 1,064 beneficiaries, without confirming the construction of
houses up to lintel level.

Clause 4.4 (Earmarking of resources) of the guidelines of IAY stipulated that
five per cent of the total allocated funds under 14 would be kept apart to
meet exigencies arising out of natural calamities and other emergent situations
like riot, arson, fire, rehabilitation under exceptional circumstances etc. with a
State-wise ceiling of 10 per cent of annual allocation (including State share).

We noticed in audit that district Gonda, Hamirpur, Mainpuri and Rampur
incurred expenditure of = 1.15 crore on natural calamities during 2009-12 but
the claims were not submitted to Gol.

The Government stated (January 2014) that the claims have been submitted.

The scheme for providing homestead sites to those rural BPL households who

effect from 24th August 2009. The required resource was to be shared equally

2.2.4 Construction of houses and facilities under convergence

2.2.4.1 Construction and location of houses

109५ dwelling units were normally to be built on individual’s plots in the main
habitation of the village. The beneficiaries had complete freedom as to the
manner of construction of the house. A Committee was to be formed to
coordinate the work if so desired. The Committee was to be sensitised to
incorporate hazard-resistant features in the design of the houses. As per
paragraph number 3.2 of guidelines, various facilities viz. sanitary latrine,
electricity connection, water supply etc. to IAY houses were to be provided
under convergence. We observed:

e the committee to sensitize beneficiaries of hazard-resistant features was
not set up in the State;

© the beneficiaries of 321'° out of 350 test-checked GPs constructed houses
in the main habitation area of the village on their own land which ensured
safety, security and social communication to the dwellers of these houses;

e a house constructed'” was demolished because the house was constructed
on the land of Revenue Department as the funds were given without
verification of sites, rendering the expenditure wasteful; and

'SAmbedkarnagar, Deoria, Fatehpur, Hamirpur, Kushinagar, Maharajganj, Mathura and Rampur out of 15 test-
checked districts did not utilise € 4.62 lakh, ₹ 3.85 lakh, € 0.91 lakh, 2.73 lakh, ₹ 1.12 lakh, ₹ 4.76 lakh, ₹ 0.20
lakh and & 0.56 lakh respectively during 2010-11

629 GPs were found with no beneficiaries.

2.2.4.2 Type Design of houses, cost effectiveness and quality of material

The State Government was to finalise type designs for 10 ४ houses along with
technical and material specifications. Under IAY, the skill up-gradation of
tural artisans and other functionaries engaged in the field of rural housing was
also to be ensured. For adoption of such technologies, necessary low cost
material was required to be developed.

We noticed in audit that the State Government did not take any initiative in
this regard. Some of the houses constructed under IAY were in dilapidated
condition. Results of beneficiary survey revealed that none of the surveyed
beneficiaries (3,126) were aware of innovative material, procurement of low
cost material, construction design of house, cost effective technology, disaster
resistant technology. Non-awareness, the income status of the targetted
beneficiaries and quantum of assistance under IAY could lead to premature
dilapidation of IAY houses.

The Government accepted (January 2014) the audit findings and stated that
mechanisms for working out type design of houses, cost effectiveness and
quality control would be permanently set up in the State.

2.2.4.3 Inadequate convergence for sanitary latrine

We noticed in audit that the activities taken up under convergence were
negligible as discussed in succeeding paragraphs:

The State Governments were to ensure dovetailing of funds under Total
Sanitation Campaign (TSC) with LAY so as to sanction IAY house and toilet
simultaneously.

We noticed in audit that the DRDAs of all the test-checked districts did not
ensure sanction of sanitary latrine while sanctioning a house under IAY.
District Panchayat Raj Officers (DPROs), the officer under whom TSC was
being implemented, were working independently and not in coordination with
the DRDAs. The DRDAs invariably did not send the list of houses sanctioned
under IAY to DPROs and also did not ensure construction of a latrine under
TSC in IAY houses. In nine!® out of 15 test-checked districts only 55,635
sanitary latrines were constructed during 2008-13, though during same period
4,41,409 houses were sanctioned under IAY. Beneficiary survey in 15 districts
revealed (April to June 2013) that the houses of 2,732 out of 3,126 JAY
beneficiaries were not provided with sanitary latrine.

2.2.4.4 Providing electric connection to IAY houses

With a view to provide comprehensive benefits to the rural BPL families, IAY
was dovetailed with Rajiv Gandhi Grameen Vidyutikaran Yojana (RGGVY).
Accordingly, DRDAs were to provide the list of LAY beneficiaries to the
implementing authorities of RGGVY and co-ordinate with them to ensure free
electric connections to JAY houses.

We noticed in audit that the DRDAs of none of the test-checked districts
forwarded the list of houses sanctioned under IAY to the offices of the Power
Department to provide electric connection to IAY houses. Beneficiary survey
revealed that 2,977 houses did not have electric supply. 3,084 beneficiaries
stated that no support from any government scheme like RGGVY was
received for electric supply.

The Government stated (January 2014) that the directives are being issued to
the districts and assured to implement the scheme in future.

2.2.4.5 Information, Education and Communication (IEC) for
convergence

In order to have active convergence, implementation of the provisions of the
IEC was essential. Further, the departments engaged to provide facilities in an
JAY house were required to be aware about the provisions of IAY. We noticed
in audit that no IEC activities were carried out in the test-checked districts.
This adversely affected the scheme in providing amenities in JAY houses.

IEC activities for convergence during audit.

2.2.5 Monitoring and evaluation

2.2.5.1 Monitoring

As per paragraph number 6.1 of IAY guidelines, monitoring was essential for
successful implementation of the scheme. The key factor in monitoring was
that the schedule of inspection was to be prescribed and all returns to be
obtained, as a part of MIS, timely and analysed to have better control over
progress of the scheme. It was to be done at all levels with special emphasis on
quality and timely completion of construction of houses.

2.2.5.2 Inefficient monitoring at State and district levels

As per paragraph no. 6.1 of IAY guidelines, the officers dealing with IAY at
the State headquarters were to visit districts regularly for satisfactory
implementation of the programme. Likewise, officers at the district and block
levels were to closely monitor all aspects of IAY by visiting work sites.
Further, the monitoring of the programme was also the responsibility of
State/district level Vigilance and Monitoring Committees (SVMCs) for Rural
Development Programmes. A monthly progress report, in respect of the Indira
Awaas Yojana, was to be furnished to Gol separately.

We noticed in audit that during 2008-13, the SVMC met only twice against the
prescribed 20 meetings. District Vigilance and Monitoring Committees in 15
test-checked districts met 46 times as against 300 meetings prescribed for
2008-13. Details of meetings held in districts are given in Appendix-2.2.10.
The State Government did not draw up schedules for inspection of houses by
officers of various levels and also did not prescribe returns/reports for better
and regular monitoring of the scheme.

2.2.5.3 Deficient grass root level monitoring

We observed:

e the schedules for the district and block level officers’ inspection of IAY
houses were not on record as one Gram Panchyat Adhikari was posted for
six to ten GPs, internal verification of work at many places was not done.

e the results of the beneficiary survey revealed that 2,091 out of 3,126
houses surveyed were not inspected. Of these, 134 beneficiaries did not get
second installments as of July 2013.

ee)


Audit Report (Gener

e a beneficiary of GP Bahera Sadat of the Block Airayan of Fatehpur did not
construct the house as of July 2013 but both the installments were credited
to her bank account in 2009-10.

e Second installments were to be released only after completing the
construction of the house up to lintel level but as can be seen in the
photograph below, in Rampur district the house was not constructed even
though, the second installment was released to the beneficiary;

Thus, the works were not verified by the concerned officers/officials before
releasing the second installment.

The Government stated (January 2014) that the beneficiary was not found in
their verification done after audit.

As per paragraph 9.3 of IAY guidelines, the State Government was to put in
place a system of quality monitoring and guide/assist the beneficiary in
achieving satisfactory quality in respect of the site selection, use of appropriate
cost effective technology, selection of design and materials for construction so
that negative environmental impact was reduced.

We noticed in audit that the State Government did not put in place a system
for quality monitoring and low/appropriate cost technology for the
construction of houses. Many houses constructed were in dilapidated condition
and without the required amenities.

The Government, while accepting the observation, assured (January 2014) to

take suitable action. The fact remains that the quality monitoring was not
ensured by the Government.

a


2.2.5.5 Monitoring through MIS (AwaasSoft)

Awaas-Soft is a local language-enabled workflow-based transaction level
Management Information System (MIS) in place since 2010-11, to facilitate
e-governance of JAY. The System was prepared and deployed at
http://iay.nic.in by National Informatics Center in collaboration with MoRD
(Gol). The System is designed for all stakeholders of IAY including the
beneficiary. The AwaasSoft has nine different modules”! designed to capture
all the transactions of the implementation of the scheme.

We noticed in audit that the MIS data on AwaasSoft was not implemented
effectively with nine different modules designed to capture all transactions
that would be required in implementation of the scheme making it difficult to
retrieve the data. Though there is a provision of four per cent under
administrative expenses for giving the IT trained manpower for this scheme,
the State Government started the use of AwaasSoft only from April 2013,
much after the introduction of MIS through AwaasSoft.

The Government accepted (January 2014) the finding and stated that use of
AwaasSoft was started from April 2013. As AwaasSoft was launched in 2010-
11, its usage should have started much before April 2013.

2.2.5.6 Transparency and grievance redressal

In order to ensure transparency in the implementation of IAY at various levels,
people were to have access to information about implementation of the
programme in all respects. Disclosure of information was to be the rule and
withholding of information, an exception.

We noticed in audit that various records and information viz., list of disaster
resistant construction features suitable for the region/locality, allocations made
to the villages under IAY, guidelines of IAY, criteria of selecting beneficiaries
etc. were not available with the GPs. Thus, the intended transparency in
implementation of the scheme was not maintained.

In reply, the State Government stated (January 2014) that the lists are placed
at public places but evidences to that effect were neither enclosed nor noticed
during audit.

2.2.5.7 Complaint monitoring system

To address complaints, paragraph no. 6.1.2 of IAY guidelines stipulated
setting up of an effective complaint monitoring system with adequate staff at
the State level. The website of the MoRD had interactive provision for filing
complaints.

* (i) Target setting for a year (ii) Beneficiary management (iii) Inspection/verification of houses (iv) Fund
Management (v) On Tine submission of demand for fund (vi) Proposal processing in Ministry of Rural Development
(vii) Personnel module (viii) Grievance redressal system (ix) Linkage with Financial Institution.

ee)


dit Report (General y ended 31 March 2013

We noticed in audit that there was no independent and specifically manned
complaint monitoring system in place at the State level. The section, dealing
with the release of State share, was also assigned the work of disposal of the
complaint cases. Out of 34 complaint cases received during 2009-13 at State
level, only three cases were disposed of. One complaint case of 2005 was
outstanding as of July 2013. At DRDA level, though general complaints were
being recorded in a common register and redressed but segregated records for
JAY specific works were not maintained. Thus, effective grievance redressal
mechanism at the State level and in the test-checked districts was absent.
Some complaint cases related to Jungal Nonia Patti, village of Kushinagar are
discussed below.

A complaint case (June 2012) of irregular sanction of IAY houses was pending
with Commissioner, Rural Development (CRD) as of July 2013.

e eighty one houses were sanctioned in February 2010 mentioning 60 BPL
family ID numbers and 73 houses were sanctioned in March 2010 against
60 BPL numbers;

e against BPL number 12,466, two houses were sanctioned mentioning ID
number 15,551 and 21,458; and

Though sanction of houses in 2009-10 to seven beneficiaries not listed in BPL
and IAY waitlists was found correct by CRD, recovery of = 1.75 lakh was not
done as of July 2013.

The Government accepted (January 2014) the facts and stated that partial
recovery (® 1.25 lakh) has been affected. The reply confirms the observation
as the final findings are still awaited.

2.2.5.8 Social Audit

Social audit, with the objective of public accountability and public vigilance,
on regular basis, was made central to the monitoring process under IAY. All
records were to be made available in ‘social audit’.

We noticed in audit that the system of social audit was not put in place by the
Government. Hence the deficiencies viz. selection of ineligible beneficiaries,
selection of non-BPL families, allotment of more than one house against the
same BPL number etc. persisted, as discussed in foregoing paragraphs, not
made known to the people. Further the results of beneficiary survey revealed
that 2,332 beneficiaries were not aware of the method of preparation of
waitlist and Gram Sabha deliberations/approval.

The Government stated (January 2014) that the decision for social audit
through the Social Audit Cell, established (April 2013) at the State level, has
been taken. The reply confirms that social audit was not taken up during
2008-13. Further, Social Audit Cell was established under MGNREGS
administrative expenditure and not from IAY fund.

2.2.5.9 Maintenance of records

The list of important records required to be maintained by DRDAs for IAY are
given in Appendix-2.2.11. We noticed, in audit, that maintenance of records in
test-checked districts was inadequate and improper as discussed below:

e None of the test-checked districts maintained convergence progress
register, schedule of inspection register, assets register, register of
advances to implementing agencies (except in Deoria and Budaun) and
register to review the progress of expenditure (except in Gonda and
Kushinagar); and

e two districts (Budaun and Fatehpur) did not maintain cash books and three
districts (Gonda, Mathura and Rampur) did not keep register of receipts.
Budaun did not maintain cheque issue register. Cheques issued by the
banks were not recorded in register of cheque books by four districts
(Budaun, Jhansi, Maharajganj and Varanasi);

e Cash book was not maintained properly in Jhansi, Hamirpur, Kushinagar,
Maharajganj and Varanasi. Grant receipt register in Budaun was not
maintained properly. Cheque issue register in Ambedkarnagar and
Hamirpur, register of cheque books in Ambedkarnagar, complaint register
in five districts (Budaun, Fatehpur, Mathura, Lucknow and Varanasi) and
the register of advances to implementing agencies were maintained either
improperly or were incomplete in Budaun.

The Government accepted (January 2014) the finding and stated that the
instructions have been issued to all the concerned officials for proper
maintenance of all records of IAY.

2.2.5.10 Impact assessment

As per paragraph no. 6.2 of IAY guidelines, Gol was to conduct periodic
evaluation studies of the implementation of IAY. The State Government was
also to conduct evaluation studies regarding the implementation and impact of
the programme on its own. The evaluation reports of the State Government
were to be furnished to Gol to take remedial actions.

Audit noticed that the State Government did not conduct any evaluation study
of the scheme. Hence, the weaknesses in implementation of the scheme were
not brought out and consequently remedial measures could not be taken.

The Government stated (January 2014) that the State Planning department

conducts evaluation study of the programme. Moreover, the State Government
did not provide any report of evaluation study.

2.2.7 Conclusion

The primary objective of IAY was to help in construction/upgradation of
dwelling units of rural BPL householders belonging to members of SC/ST,
free bonded labourers, minorities and other non-SC/ST by providing them
financial assistance which was only partially achieved.

System and procedures followed were deficient due to non-preparation of
database leading to incorrect identification of beneficiaries. Resource
allocation by the districts was not based on rural housing shortages and SC/ST
population. Fixation of unrealistic targets led to downward revision of targets.
Ineligible beneficiaries were sanctioned houses under the scheme. Houses
were sanctioned in the name of male members of the families having female
members. The scheme suffered cuts not only due to short release of State share
but also due to higher unspent balances. Considerable targets vis-a-vis funds
were set apart for villages identified in State Government’s schemes which
violated the IAY guidelines. Substantial funds were not only parked in IAY
bank accounts but also kept out of IAY bank accounts.

Due to sanctioning of houses without ascertaining Bank account number of the
beneficiaries, the actual and timely credit to the beneficiaries’ accounts was

also not ensured. Funds spent on
which reimbursement of the sa
scheme did not yield the desired
resistant technologies were not

natural calamities remained unclaimed due to
me by Gol was not made. Homestead site
result in the State. Type designs and disaster
prescribed. Facilities under convergence like

sanitary latrines, electricity connections, insurance and job card under
MGNREGS were largely not provided. Grievance redressal mechanism was
absent. Social audit was not being conducted. Scheme was not evaluated by
the State Government.

Thus, the objective of providing housing to the rural poor was not achieved to
the envisaged extent due to weaknesses at different stages of planning,
implementation and monitoring of the scheme.

2.2.8 Recommendations

The Government should ensure that:

e A proper database for housing shortage and beneficiaries under each
category is maintained and houses under IAY are sanctioned as per

seniority fixed in LAY waitlists.

e After receipt of targets from Gol, the resource allocation is made as per
guidelines. In making the annual plan, a bottom-up approach is adopted.

e To avoid financial cuts by Gol, the closing balances are within the
prescribed limit and due State share are released and on time.

e Bank account numbers of beneficiaries are ensured before sanctioning of
the house and the second installment is released only after the receipt of

verification certificate.

e Area-specific designs for IAY houses and low cost and disaster-resistant
technologies for the construction of houses are developed.

e Facilities under convergence are provided to the beneficiaries.

e Public accountability in implementation of IAY through in-built
monitoring mechanism is ensured.

e@ Grievance redressal mechanism is developed and is implemented
effectively. Social audit is put in place.

e@ The scheme is evaluated from time to time for impact assessment.


MEDICAL EDUCATION DEPARTMENT

2.3 Functioning of Moti Lal Nehru Medical College, Allahabad and
Associated Teaching Hospitals

Executive Summary

The Government established Moti Lal Nehru Medical College at Allahabad
in 1961 to provide medical education, research, training and treatment to the
ailing persons. A performance audit revealed the following:

e No Plan was prepared for timely implementation of Centrally Sponsored
Schemes which resulted in inordinate delays in their implementation.

e Unrecognised Post Graduate degree and diploma courses were offered
by the College. Neither College Council was constituted nor was
Central laboratory functional to take up research activities.

Government should take effective steps for recognition of courses.

e Though the number of patients in IPDs and OPDs was increasing and
there were more than one patient per bed in emergency ward of SNCH,
there were shortages of specialists/consultant doctors and nursing staff.

Government should ensure to fill the vacant posts.

e Swaroop Rani Nehru Hospital did not provide benefit of free treatment
to the patients living below poverty line. Specialised services in
Gastroenterology and Cardiology departments remained inadequate for
want of AMC of equipment, approval of proposals for civil works etc.

Government should establish a mechanism to ensure free treatment of
BPL patients and enhance the adequacy of services by clearing the
pending proposals.

e Fifteen departments of the College lacked equipment. Besides, some
equipment (₹ 1.49 crore) were non-functional.

e There was loss of revenue of € 36 lakh during 2008-11, as the area for a
chemist shop in the premises of SRNH was not provided to the firm.

e Monitoring was inadequate and incorrect figures were reported to the
Government of Uttar Pradesh.

2.3.1 Introduction

Moti Lal Nehru Medical College, Allahabad (MLNMC), one of the nine
Medical Colleges in the State, was established in 1961 by the State
Government with the objective to impart medical education, conduct research,
organise training and provide medical services to the ailing persons. Earlier
(till 15 July 2008) it was affiliated to Allahabad University but later it was
affiliated (since 16 July 2008) to the Chhatrapati Shahuji Maharaj Medical

>


dit Report (General y ended 31 March 2013

University (CSMMU), Lucknow. To impart medical education and confer
academic degrees’, there are 25 academic departments (Appendix-2.3.1) and
three Associated Teaching Hospitals (ATHs) under MLNMC.

All the three ATHs namely (i) Swaroop Rani Nehru Hospital (SRNH) (ii)
Sarojini Naidu Children Hospital (SNCH) and (iii) Manohar Das Eye Hospital
(MDEH) under MLNMC are located in Allahabad city. Together, there are 13
Out Patient Departments (OPDs)’ and 10 Indoor Patient Departments (IPDs)’;
their bed capacity was 1080 beds (SRNH: 852, SNCH: 120 and MDEH: 108)
in March 2013.

The campus of MLNMC, occupies an area of 59.20 acres which includes
teaching departments, library, administrative departments, boys’ and girls’
hostels, residences for faculty members, guest house, alumni building, sports
field etc. The three ATHs including OPDs, IPDs and OTs etc. together occupy
41.04 acres’.

2.3.2 Organisational structure

Principal Secretary, Medical Education, Government of Uttar Pradesh (GoUP)
and Director General, Medical Education and Training, Lucknow (DGMET),
are overall in-charge at the Government and Departmental levels. The
Principal, MLNMC is responsible for the administrative & financial
management relating to admission, examination and other academic activities,
purchase of medicines, equipment and delivery of services by three ATHs etc.
MLNMC comprises 25 departments and each department is run under a Head
of Department (Appendix-2. 3.2).

2.3.3. Audit objectives

The objectives of the Performance Audit were to assess and ascertain whether:

e there was adequate planning for academics, research, training of doctors
and delivery of health & medical services;

e the delivery of services, health & medical, were appropriate and efficient;

e the Centrally Sponsored Schemes (CSSs) were being implemented as
envisaged;

e the funds were sanctioned, released and utilised economically and
effectively in adherence to the extant Government and other rules;

e the manpower and administrative management were efficient, economical
and effective; and

1 Bachelor of Medicine and Bachelor of Surgery (MBBS), Doctor of Medicine (MD), Master of Surgery (MS) and
Doctorate of Medicine (DM).

3 SRNH: 8; SNCH: 1; and MDEH: 1

+ SRNH: 31.06 acres; SNCH: 7.41 acres; and MDEH: 2.57 acres.

Chapter 2 - Performance Audit

e the internal controls, monitoring and evaluation mechanisms were in place,
adequate and working effectively.

The audit criteria have been derived from the following sources:

e Guidelines issued by the Medical Council of India (MCI) for 100
admissions Regulations, 1999 and for Post Graduate Medical Education
Regulations, 2000;

e Financial rules and regulations framed by GoUP; and

e Instructions issued by GoUP from time to time.

The records of all the departments of MLNMC, Allahabad and three ATHs
for the period 2008-13 were scrutinised during April 2013 to July 2013 in
the offices of the Principal Secretary, Medical Education, GoUP, Lucknow;
Director General, Medical Education & Training (DGMET); Principal and
Finance Controller, MLNMC; Superintendent in Chief (SIC), SRNH;
Director, MDEH; and Head of SNCH.

An entry conference was held with Principal Secretary, Medical Education on
20 March, 2013 wherein the audit objectives, audit criteria and audit scope and
methodology were discussed and inputs obtained. Exit conference was held
with Special Secretary, Medical Education on 23 January, 2014. Replies
furnished by the Government have been incorporated suitably.

Further, joint physical verifications and interviews with the beneficiaries were
also conducted and photographic evidences were obtained in support of
observations.

The cooperation extended by the Government; Directorate; Principal,
MLNMC; SIC, SRNH; Director, MDEH; and Head of SNCH during audit is
acknowledged.

The audit findings are discussed hereinafter:

The objective of MLNMC is to impart health & medical education; conduct
research in medical and associated sciences; impart training to the doctors and
para-medical staff; and to deliver health & medical services to the people of
Allahabad and adjoining districts/areas. We observed that:

e MLNMC and its ATHs functioned without any strategic, perspective or
annual plans for academics, research and training to the doctors and


dit Report (General y ended 31 March 2013

para-medical staff at the levels of GOUP, DGMET during 2008-13, although
it was responsible to cater to the tertiary health & medical needs of a large
population of Allahabad and adjoining districts/areas.

e No plans were made to increase the intake capacity of MLNMC. The
intake capacity of MLNMC was 50 students in 1961 which was raised to
100 students in 1966. In July 2013, it was increased to 150.

e No plan was in place to augment infrastructural capacities of MLNMC to
improve quality of education and delivery of services.

e MLNMC and its ATHs received funds from GoUP under plan and non-
plan heads. During the period 2008-13 the plan heads accounted for only
15 per cent of the total allotments and the remaining 85 per cent was for
the non-plan heads to meet expenditure on revenue heads.

e No plan for putting an efficient mechanism was in place for effective
implementation of State/Centrally Sponsored Schemes (९588) viz.
Strengthening/up-gradation of MLNMC, Strengthening of Cancer Unit,
Trauma Centre and Janani Suraksha Yojana during 2008-13 as discussed
in paragraph number 2.3.13.

Inreply, the Government stated (January 2014) that plans were made and trauma
services were available in the hospital. Reply was not acceptable as no plans were
made and submitted to DGMET/Government during 2008-13 and trauma
services were not available due to non-completion of civil work and equipment of
trauma centre were lying unpacked.

Thus, in order to achieve the avowed objectives, a Perspective Plan and
Annual Plans should be prepared keeping in view the growing health and
medical care needs of the people for effective and qualitative tertiary medical
care and also to promote research for enhancing the quality of human life.

2.3.8 Academics and research activities

In exercise of the powers conferred by Section 33 of the Indian Medical
Council Act, 1956, MCI framed the ‘Minimum Standard Requirements for the
Medical College for 100 Admissions Annually Regulations, 1999’ (MCT
Regulations 1999) and the ‘Post Graduate Medical Education Regulations,
2000’ for grant of recognition to medical institutions fulfilling the prescribed.
requirements in terms of departments, equipment etc.

MLNMC offers degree courses at graduate & post graduate levels; and post
graduate diploma & super specialisation courses (Appendix-2.3.3). The
Graduate Level Course is a bachelor’s degree in Medical Sciences, the Post
Graduate Course is a master’s degree in Medical Science and the Post
Graduate Diploma Course is a diploma at master’s level. The degrees for
MBBS and Doctor of Medicine (MD) & Master of Surgery (MS) courses are
conferred by CSMMU, Lucknow.

the Government order dated 15 May 1964, and were ineligible to render
services outside UP. Jn reply, the Government admitted (January 2014) the
facts.

2.3.8.3 Lecture theatres

MCI prescribed (1999) the number and capacity of the lecture theatres and
also equipment for Medical Colleges. A minimum of three gallery type lecture
theatres, two of these three with seating capacity of 120 students each and the
third with seating capacity of 250 students were required.

MLNMC, during 2008-13, had two lecture theatres for 100 students each and
two for 150 students each but no lecture theatre was available for seating 250
students. Thus, the college functioned without adequate infrastructure
facilities. In reply, the Government offered (January 2014) no comments

2.3.8.4 Deficient teaching schedule

As per appendix ‘C’ of MCI’s Regulations on Graduate Medical Education
1997, minimum teaching hours for entire semester were for pre-clinical
subjects® - 1,430 hours, para clinical subjects’ - 1,150 hours and clinical
subjects® - 1,440 hours.

We observed that the teaching schedule for graduate courses was prescribed.
for the syllabus in MCI norms. Topics were divided and allotted to available
faculties but lecture hours for individual topics were neither prescribed nor
indicated in the schedule prepared by the MLNMC. Hence, it was not
ascertained whether MCI prescribed hours were adhered to in MLNMC and its
ATHs during 2008-13.

In reply, the Government stated (January 2014) that teaching was imparted as
per norms. Reply was not acceptable as course curriculum for minimum teaching
hours, as envisaged in the MCI norms, was not prepared.

2.3.8.5 Research and College Council

One of the main objectives of MLNMC is to pursue and encourage research in the
field of health & medical science and organise training/ workshops/ seminars/
conferences etc. to keep the medical professionals, both students and faculty
abreast of new developments and technologies. MCI also emphasised the need
and recommended for a College Council (Council) for drawing up the
curriculum, training programme etc.

Research

In terms of Paragraph A.1.19 (b) of the MCI Regulations 1999, there shall be
one well equipped Central Research Laboratory (CRL) under the control of
the Dean/ Principal.

We observed that CRL was not functional in the College. Further, no budget
was allocated for research and training activities during 2008-13. No training
programmes, seminars, workshops, conferences etc. were held in MLNMC or
its ATHs from its own budget during the said period. Further, absence of
budget for research and CRL denied both the faculty and students of
prescribed practical learning and research.

In reply, the Government accepted (January 2014) that finds were being sought
for upgradation of CRL. It further stated that research was a part of the
curriculum of the post graduates (PG) and so separate allocation of budget is not
required. The reply was not acceptable as research was a post PG activity and
CRL was required to undertake research activities

College Council

MCI prescribed? a Council comprising heads of the department as members
and Principal/Dean as Chairperson. The Council was to draw up the
curriculum and training programmes and regularly organise inter-departmental
meetings for periodical research review which in turn monitored their quality.
Besides, disciplinary and other academic matters were also to be enforced by
the Council. The Council was to meet at least four times in a year. However,
no Council was constituted during 2008-13 and hence the intended benefits of
the Council were denied and quality was not ensured.

In reply, the Government stated (January 2014) that the College Council exists
and meets whenever required. However, the Principal had already accepted in
July 2013 the fact of non-existence of College Council.

2.3.9.12 Telemedicine Unit

With a view to provide specialists’ advice to the people living in remote areas,
GoUP established a Telemedicine Unit in August 2010. The Unit was
equipped with a server, Personal Computer client, scanner, camera, speaker
etc., supplied by Sanjay Gandhi Post Graduate Institute, Lucknow under
National Rural Health Mission (NRHM). But the unit was not functional for
specialists’ advice, as envisaged, since July 2011 due to expiry of tenure of
technical staff of the firm. A proposal, to re-deploy the technical staff, was
sent to DGMET (June 2012) which was pending as of January 2014 and no
pursuance was made by the Principal after June 2012. In reply, the Government
accepted (January 2014) the facts.

and the said proposal of MDEH was still not approved by GoUP.

2.3.12 Improper management of Hospital Waste
2.3.12.1 BioMedical Waste
The Bio-Medical Waste (BMW) Management and Handling Rules, 1998,
require institutions, generating BMW and hospital waste, to obtain
authorisation from the State Pollution Control Board (SPCB). The Rules also
require hospitals not to mix different categories of BMWs and to keep
different categories in separate coloured bins at the source of generation. The

category-wise quantity of BMW generated and their disposal was to be
forwarded to SPCB in a prescribed proforma in January each year.

We observed that the Principal did not obtain the requisite authorisation from
SPCB during 2008-13, except for the period from August to December 2011
for only SRNH. The required information was not sent to SPCB in January
each year (2008-13). SPCB also did not inspect the BMW disposal
mechanisms of the MLNMC and its ATHs during 2008-13.

In reply, the Government stated (January 2014) that compliance of the BMW
handling rules, has been initiated. The fact remains that BMW was not treated as
per SPCB Rules during 2008-13 putting the lives of patients, doctors, para
medical staff etc. at risk.

Sanitation work in MLNMC and associated SRNH and SNCH was entrusted
to private firms during 2008-13. Scrutiny revealed that the firms did not carry
out the sanitation works as per the terms of the agreement*®, Less number of

* Deployment of agreed number of workers for mechanised/manual cleaning with material.

Oa


‘formance Audit

Chapter

workers were deployed in SNCH; absence of workers during evening and
night shifts was noticed; irregular monitoring by the supervisor was done; and
less usage of cleaning materials — broom, phenyl etc. was done. All these
reasons resulted in toilets, wards, rooms, corridors, lawn etc. being very dirty
and grossly unhygienic during 2008-13.

Street dogs and other pets
were found to be loitering in =e 5 June 2013
the wards and other parts of हि. | ——

In reply, the Government stated (January 2014) that Government employees were
also deployed to carry out the sanitation work and sewer line of SRNH was
functional. Reply was not acceptable as sanitation arrangements in SRNH and
SNCH were evidently inadequate and proper monitoring and supervision was not
being done as prescribed in the agreements.

2.3.14.3 Operation of Account without authority

The State Financial Rules forbid retention of Government money outside the
Government account. We observed that the Principal, MLNMC and
Superintendent-in-Chief (SIC), SRNH were vested with the power of Drawing
and Disbursing Officer (DDO) upto July 2010. In July 2010, power of DDO of
SIC was withdrawn and vested with the Principal, MLNMC. SIC had been
operating a current account in a bank’ for deposit of various receipts mainly
electric charges received from the employees and user charges received from
patients and withdrew from this account for hospital expenditure such as
petrol etc. Further, it was noticed that at the time of withdrawal of the powers
of DDO of the SIC, ₹ 30 lakh was lying unutilised in the said account.
However, after July 2010, without the powers of DDO, SIC continued to
operate as DDO and adjusted withdrawals against the regular budget”, even
without the approval of the Principal, DDO.

Further, on 19 June 2013, there was हैं 34.38 lakh in the aforesaid account,
which included € 10.41 lakh without any detail. We observed that SIC, SRNH
not only operated the account without any authority but also retained funds
outside the Government account, which was fraught with the risk of
misuse/misappropriation. In reply, the Government stated (January 2014) that
the account was used in emergency. Reply was not acceptable as SIC, SRNH, not
being DDO, was not authorised to operate the account even in emergency.

from the patients but did not hand it over to the head cashier.

2.3.14.5 Inadequate mobilisation of resources

As envisaged under NRHM framework, in case of Rogi Kalyan Samitis (RKS),
a large part of the non-tax receipts are to be retained by the hospital(s). This
acts as an incentive for the hospital(s) to mobilise resources for the purposes
specified by the Government. GoUP directed (March 2011) the Principal to
send a proposal to constitute RKS in MLNMC and utilise the user charges
collected from the patients. However, the proposal was not sent by the
Principal as of January 2014. This reflected lackadaisical attitude of MLNMC
and its ATHs to activities which generated non-tax receipts as discussed in
succeeding paragraphs.

Failure to recover rent

GoUP decided (August 2008) to provide two pieces of land in the premises of
SRNH for three years, one each adjacent to the OPD and the IPD, to a private
person to operate private chemist shops. The chemist was to pay monthly
licence fee of = one lakh per shop and provide medicines, free of cost, to poor
ailing persons upto % one lakh or 0.5 per cent of the monthly sale, whichever
was higher and a committee was to be constituted by the Principal to ensure
this.

We observed that the Principal executed (December 2008) Memorandum of
Understanding (MoU) with a firm® for providing area for two shops for three
years in the premises of SRNH. However, the land for one shop was not made
available, resulting in loss of revenue of हैं 36 lakh>* during December, 2008-
11. In December 2011, the said MoU expired but the firm continued to occupy
the shop unauthorisedly till date (January 2014). Neither the MoU was
renewed for about two years nor the committee, envisaged in the MoU, was
constituted to ensure the stipulated help to poor ailing persons. This chemist
shop was unauthorisedly operating in the premises of SRNH paying the old
rate of rent fixed in December 2008 and not providing, at least in terms of
amount, the envisaged help to poor ailing persons.

In reply, the Government, without providing the copy of Judgement, stated
(January 2014) that the three shops were not vacated due to stay order of Hon'ble
Supreme Court.

Failure to assess royalty

GoUP decided (August 2003) to provide a piece of land to a firm® for ten
years for installation of CT Scan Machine in SRNH. The firm was to charge
fee ranging from हैं 500 to हैं 3,200 for various types of 31 scans and pay
royalty at the rate of 10 per cent of the fee charged to SRNH. Fifteen per cent
of the total scans were to be done free of cost for poor ailing persons. Besides,
a fine of % 1,000 per day was to be imposed for down time period. The
Principal was to constitute a committee for monitoring and inspections.

DGMET entered (August 2003) into an agreement with the firm. Pursuant to
the agreement, SIC, SRNH provided a piece of land (approximately 3,200
square feet) to the firm in SRNH for installation of CT Scan machine. During
January 2009 to March 2013, the firm reported to have performed 3,711 scans
and paid ₹ 2.88 lakh to SIC, SRNH. However, the number of scans actually
performed were never verified by SIC, SRNH. Besides, SIC, SRNH did not
maintain any record of poor patients referred to by the doctors of the SRNH
for free scans to be performed by the firm. Hence, neither the actual number of
scans performed nor actual number of free scans done during 2008-2013 were
authenticated in audit. Further, fine of = 1.20 lakh at the rate of = 1,000 per
day was not imposed on the firm for down period of four months (October
2009, November 2009, July 2012 and August 2012). The Principal did not
constitute the envisaged committee for monitoring and inspection of the
scanning facility to ensure that the due royalty was being received by
MLNMC and 15 per cent of the total scans were being done free of cost for
poor ailing persons.

The Government stated (January 2014) that matter was being examined.

Irregular running of a Canteen

A Canteen in the SRNH was running in four rooms of about 90 sqm. No
documents were furnished, though called for. We observed that this canteen
was running prior to 2003, as on an application*” made in 2003 for permission
for running the canteen, the then Principal accorded approval without entering
into any agreement with terms and conditions. No rent was ever paid (July
2013) and electricity & water charges of हैं 1.56 lakh were outstanding.
Though the canteen was running irregularly, neither any notice was served nor
FIR lodged. No effort was made to disconnect supplies of electricity and
water.

In reply, the Government stated (January 2014) that no unauthorised canteen
was running in SRNH. It is amess of junior doctors residing in old PG Hostel and
notice has been given to close the mess. The reply was not acceptable as
documents furnished during audit revealed that it is a private canteen and not a
mess for junior doctors,

2.3.15 Establishment
Employees Provident Fund

Para 29 of Employees’ Provident Fund Scheme, 1952 provides that
Employees’ Provident Fund (EPF) contribution at the rate of 13.61 per cent
should be deposited into the EPF accounts of the employees. Accordingly,
EPF contribution of the employees, deputed on contract basis through firms
for security and sanitary work in MLNMC, were either to be deposited by the
employer (firm) or by the Principal.

Scrutiny revealed that हैं 1.98 crore was paid to various firms as salary of the
employees deployed through these firms on contractual basis for security and
sanitary works during 2008-12 but EPF contribution of & 0.27 crore®® was not
paid to the firms by MLNMC. This made MLNMC responsible for payment of
contributions of EPF. MLNMC had not taken any step to ensure/enforce the
payment of EPF. As such MLNMC failed to discharge its obligatory duty.
In reply, the Government admitted (January 2014) the facts.

To establish CMRS for exchange of patients’ information among various
departments and for recording the case history of patients ([PDs) in SRNH,
GoUP sanctioned (December 2011) हैं 15 lakh for purchase of 15 computers
with a server including software and networking arrangements.

We observed that the Principal without inviting tenders, in disregard to
GoUP’s Order (September 2008), placed (30 March, 2012) an order for 20

The Government stated (January 2014) that proposal of software was under
consideration. No comment was offered about the dates of supply order, delivery
and payment and idle computers.

2.3.17 Sensitivity to error signals

To make MLNMC and its ATHs sensitive to the health & medical needs of the
people it was imperative that a system to detect error signals from various
sources like deliberations of the monthly monitoring meetings at different
levels; implementation of Right To Information (RTI) Act, 2005; internal
audit; and print and electronic media etc. was put in place at the level of the
Government, Department, MLNMC and its ATHs. We observed that:

e As per section 5 (1) of RTI Act, 2005, a Public Information Officer (PIO)
should be designated by each public office for providing information
sought by the applicants within 30 days from the date of receiving the
application. However, the PIO was not designated in SNCH as of July
2013. In MDEH, SRNH and MLNMC, PIOs were designated (in
December 2008, December 2011 and December 2012 respectively) with
delays of over three, six and seven years respectively. Consequently, 60
applications” were lying unattended" as of June 2013.

e No Grievance Redressal Cell was constituted in MLNMC. No adequate
forum to register and redress the grievances of the patients and students
was in place.

e No Internal Audit of MLMNC and its ATHs was conducted by DGMET
during 2008-13.

e No mechanism was evolved to take cognizance of the print and electronic
media reports and act upon it, if required.

e College council was not constituted (paragraph number 2.3.8.5).

In reply, the Government, without providing any evidence, stated (January
2014) that Grievance Redressal Cell was functional and cognizance of the

2.3.18 Evidencing and documentation

During review of Functioning of MLNMC & other ATHs we observed that no
records relating to free medical services provided to the people living below
poverty line and patients visiting the hospitals were maintained by the ATHs;
Doctor-wise details of number of OPD patients were also not maintained; and
records relating to waiting periods for surgery in respect of the indoor patients
were not captured. Further, no records relating to mortality review committee
meetings were produced. Consequently, the reasons for the deaths could not
be ascertained in audit.

Inreply, the Government, without providing any evidence, stated (January 2014)
that records were being maintained and mortality committee meeting was held.
Reply was not acceptable as it was contradictory to reply given earlier and
further, no such records were maintained during 2008-13. No records relating to
mortality committee meeting were produced during audit.

2.3.19 Monitoring and Evaluation
Monthly review meetings at the Government level

Progress of on-going schemes and other patient care related activities of
MLNMC and its ATHs are monitored in the monthly review meetings of all
Heads of the Departments of Medical Education at Government level, which
are attended by the Principals of Medical Colleges of the State. We observed
that 15 against stipulated 60 meetings were held during 2008-13. Further,
cases of incorrect reporting were noticed in audit. OT register of the SNCH
revealed only 199 procedures between August 2010 and October 2012 but 823
was reported to the Government in Monthly Progress Reports (MPRs) during
the same period. Likewise, 208 procedures” were reported to have been
performed by a surgeon between August 2011 and October 2011, whose
contract had expired in July 2011. Similarly, incomes of € 4,500 and % 6,000
were reported in MPRs in August 2012 and September 2012 respectively from
CT Scan as against Nil and हैं 480 during the same months. We noticed
differences in figures reported in audit (434 scan) and to the Legislative
Assembly of Uttar Pradesh (723 scan) regarding number of free CT Scans
performed by the firm® between April 2009 and June 2012 for the poor
people. Evidently, progress reports were neither scrutinized at the level of
DGMET nor at the Government level. Thus, monitoring was ineffective.
Follow up action on the instructions of the earlier Monthly Review Meetings,
held at the Government’s level, was not ensured by the DGMET/GoUP in the
subsequent meetings.

© 70 in August, 67 in September and 71 in October.

Jai Durga Diagnostic Centre, Allahabad.

Audit Report (Gener:

In reply, Principal MLNMC accepted (July 2013) the facts and figures. The
Government offered no specific comment.

Monthly meetings at the level of MLNMC

In order to avoid clash between resident doctors and patients’ relatives and to
maintain harmony in the ATHs, GoUP issued (May 2011) instructions to hold
monthly meetings of HoDs with resident doctors. Further, the Principal was
required to hold monthly meetings with HoDs and submit report to GoUP.
However, we observed that only one meeting, with all the faculty members,
was called (on 3 March, 2010) by the Principal.

In reply, the Principal, MLNMC, without furnishing any evidence, stated
(July 2013) that records relating to meeting of HoDs with resident doctors was
maintained at HoDs level. The Government stated (January 2014) that monthly
meeting was called every month. Reply was not acceptable as it was contrary to
the facts produced during audit and the records available.

2.3.20 Conclusion

MLNMC and its ATHs were set up in 1961 to cater to the growing health and
medical care needs of the people for effective and qualitative tertiary care by
imparting health & medical education, promoting research and delivering
quality health and medical services to the people for enhancing the quality of
human life.

But the desirable perspective and annual plans were not prepared. Academics
and research suffered for reasons of non-recognition of courses by MCT;
inadequate lecture theatres; deficient teaching schedules; insufficient faculty
members and nursing staff; non-functional Central Research Laboratory,
College Council and non-allocation of funds for research. The quality and.
quantity of health and medical services delivered were compromised as
doctor-patient ratio was skewed among doctors; allotment of beds was
irrational; medicines were not subjected to required testing; diets were not
prescribed by the doctors; ambulances were often used for administrative
work; envisaged free treatment of patients, with specified diseases, living
below poverty line was not ensured etc. The existing equipment were not
always functional and AMCs for maintenance of equipment were not
executed. Super speciality centres did not come of their own for want of
personnel, equipment and funds. Civil works were largely behind schedule and
Centrally Sponsored Schemes were not well-implemented. Bio-medical &
hospital wastes were not taken care of, as required, and sanitation, hygiene,
protection/prevention against/of fire, security etc. were not ensured. Financial
management including mobilisation of resources for Rogi Kalyan Samiti was
inadequate. Computerisation was not extended the desired attention, internal
control was weak and so were evidencing- documentation and monitoring and
evaluation.

O—


formance Audit

2.3.21 Recommendations
The Government should ensure that:

¢ Perspective and annual plans are prepared for academics, research and
quantity and quality of medical & health services - together with the
required associated activities to merit the MCI recognition.

¢ Equipment, faculty members and nursing staff are adequate in number and
quality to achieve the MCI norms.

e Bio-medical and hospital wastes are handled as required together with
proper sanitation, hygiene, security and fire protection/prevention
services.

¢ Financial management is economical, efficient and effective and adhering
to the extant rules.

e Resources are mobilized for Rogi Kalyan Samitis, monitoring and
evaluation are effective and evidencing and documentation are proper.

ECHNICAL EDUCA’ DEPARTMENT

In 1962, the existing Civil Engineering School (established in 1955) at
Allahabad was designated as Allahabad Polytechnic for diploma courses in
engineering. In 1981, the Polytechnic was upgraded as a non-Government
aided institution and designated as the Institute of Engineering and Rural
Technology (IERT), Allahabad. In 2001, IERT commenced degree courses in
engineering on self-financing basis and pay and allowances to its academic
and non-academic staff was to be met out of the fee realised from the students.
Thus, IERT comprised two divisions namely, diploma division, funded by the
State Government, and self-financed degree division.

The organisational structure of IERT is given below:

Principal Secretary (Technical Education)
(Overall contro] at the Government level)

v
Director, Directorate (Technical Education)
(Overall control at the State level)

v

Regional, Joint Director (Technical Education)

(Control at the regional level)

y

Authorised Controller (Commissioner, Allahabad)

(Controlling Officer)
v
Director, IERT, Allahabad
(Administration and financial management)

The Director, IERT was assisted by the academic and non-academic staff of
three categories!.

Records relating to the financial management of IERT for 2003-13 were
scrutinised in the office of the Director, IERT, Allahabad during April to June
2013. The audit objective was to examine the financial management of the
Institute. The results of examination are set out in the succeeding paragraphs:


formance Audit

2.4.2. Audit findings
2.4.2.1 Governing Body of IERT

To supervise and control the affairs, the Memorandum of Association (MOA)
Form of accounts was not of IERT provided for a thirteen-member Governing Body. As per para (XVII)
prescribed even after 20 of MOA the Governing Body, constituted in July 1982, was to prescribe forms
years of taking over the 6
management, of accounts for approval by the State Government. However, no such forms of
accounts were prepared by the institute upto September 1992. In October
1992, the Chairman, Management Committee, IERT, Allahabad recommended
to the Government to take over its management for no reasons on record.
Accepting (November 1992) the recommendation, the Government dissolved
the Governing Body on 4 November 1992 and appointed Controlling Officers
for various periods” as of June 2013.

Audit observed that the Government did not take any action for prescribing
forms of accounts for IERT as envisaged in the para (XVII) of MOA even
after 20 years of taking over the management. During discussion (January 2014),
the Government accepted it.

2.4.2.2. Financial Management

The Government of Uttar Pradesh provides grant-in-aid? to IERT for its
Diploma Division and the Government of India provides grant-in-aid for
various projects of IERT. Technical Education department issues sanctions for
grant-in-aid to the Director, Technical Education, Uttar Pradesh which is
further released to the Regional Joint Director, Varanasi‘, Bills are prepared
by the Director, IERT, Allahabad for submission to the Regional Joint
Director to disburse pay and allowances etc. Besides, IERT also collects fee
from the students of the diploma courses. Funds, thus received, were spent on
the disbursement of pay and allowances to the academic and non-academic
staff of the diploma courses, maintenance of assets etc. The fee collected from
the students of the degree courses under the self-financing scheme constituted
receipts of the degree courses which is spent on disbursement of pay and
allowances to its academic and non-academic staff. Further, 20 per cent of
such receipts were also to be utilized on the disbursement of pay and
allowances to academic and non-academic staff of the diploma courses as per
the Government’s instruction issued in June 2009.

2.4.2.3, Multiplicity of bank accounts and un-reconciled differences

The Memorandum of Association of IERT did not specify the number of bank
accounts to be opened for funds received from the State Government,
Government of India or from any other source. However, in response to an
audit query, the Director intimated (July 2013) that bank account was to be
opened after the approval of the Governing Body/Controlling Officers
appointed by the Government since November 1992.

Audit noticed that as many as 15 current accounts* were opened in two banks.
Out of these, 10 were operational? and the remaining five were dormant’? for
three to six years. The periodical bank reconciliation statements for 2003-13
were not maintained except for 2011-12 and there was difference of ¥ 54.34
lakh" between the bank accounts and the cash book balances as on 31 March
2012 (the Annual Accounts for 2012-13 were under finalisation).

The maintenance of 15 bank accounts coupled with unreconciled differences
of रैं 54.34 lakh at the close of March 2012 led to a maze of transactions
making trailing and examination difficult. Moreover, these bank accounts
were opened without the required approval of the Governing Body/
Controlling Officer. During discussion (January 2014), the Government
accepted the facts.

2.4.2.4 Multiplicity of Cash Books and their improper maintenance

In every office, there should be a cash book for recording day to day
transactions. Paragraph 27 A of the Financial Hand Book, Volume V,
Part I provides that a cash book should be kept in every office for recording all
moneys received and disbursements made. The Financial Rules further
provide that the cash book should be closed and balanced each day which
should be initialled by Head of the Office and the balance at the end of each
month should be verified and a certificate to that effect recorded in the cash
book under the signature of the head of the office.

We noticed that 15 subsidiary cash books were maintained. During 2003-12
neither the monthly closing of the cash books, receipts: ¥ 126 crore and
disbursements: ~ 124.40 crore, was carried out by the Director, IERT, who
was the Drawing and Disbursing Officer, nor were the over writings, cuttings
ete. therein authenticated. During discussion (January 2014), the Government
accepted the facts.

2.4.2.9 Financial Statement

Financial statement irregularity, if not disclosed, amounts to a deliberate
There was mis match misrepresentation of the financial condition of an enterprise accomplished
in the financial data. through intentional mis-statement or omission of amounts of disclosures in the
financial statements to deceive statement users. The following irregularities
were noticed:

e The total tuition fee realized (2003-13), as per the fee structure and
information furnished to audit, was ~ 9.31 crore. The tuition fee, as
reported to the Department of Technical Education Government/
Directorate of Technical Education, was हैं 9.40 crore and as reflected in
the Annual Account it was हैं 7.53 crore. The Directorate did not reconcile
the differences as of January 2014. The Director, IERT accepted
(July 2013) that there was mismatch.

e During 2003-13, IERT received हैं 7.10 lakh as retundable caution money
from the students. During the corresponding period, only = 700 was refunded
during 2008-09 and 2009-10, leaving an accumulated balance of % 7.09 lakh,
as of March 2013. However, as against the accumulated balance of
% 7.09 lakh, only हैं 1.81 lakh was disclosed in the Annual Account

(March 2012), The Director, IERT stated (July 2013) that the mismatch
would be reconciled. Thus, the financial statements did not reflect the true
and fair picture of the financial position. During discussion (January 2014),
the Government accepted the observation.

2.42.10 Other interesting points

e In the Institute, neither the post of Deputy Registrar was sanctioned nor
the proposal for the same was sent to the Government as of May 2013.
However, a permanent senior clerk of diploma division, in the pay scale
of € 2,200-4,000, was promoted to the post of Deputy Registrar in the pay
scale of = 6,500-10,500. During April 2006 to February 2013, the senior
clerk received additional pay and allowances of हैं 8.67 lakh in lieu of
discharging the duties as Deputy Registrar. Director, IERT stated (July
2013) that the senior clerk was discharging the duties of Deputy Registrar
with the approval of the Commissioner, Allahabad Division (Controlling
Officer). The fact remains that the post was not created by the
Government and the Commissioner, Allahabad was not competent to
create the post.

e Director, IERT invested हैं six crore in 19 fixed deposit receipts during
March 2010 to February 2012 for varying periods and earned interest of
% 5.94 lakh. The investments were made without the approval by the
Commissioner, Allahabad Division, the controlling officer. The Director

O—$



Chapter formance Audit

stated that it was his discretion. The reply was incorrect because the
investment could only be made with the approval of the controlling officer
as per the order of the Commissioner (May 2010).

e As per Para 327 and 447 of Financial Hand Book Volume VI, works
should be commenced only after estimates are prepared and technical and
administrative sanctions are accorded. The works are to be measured after
their completion. However, TI of € 18.75 lakh was sanctioned (2009-10) to
a Junior Engineer for petty works such as white washing, repair of
boundary wall etc. without preparing estimates and technical sanctions. An
amount of = 15.25 lakh was incurred during 2009-11 by him for these
works without taking measurement. Remaining ~ 3.50 lakh was not
recovered from him even after lapse of more than two years. The Director
agreed to the audit observations and stated that the rules will be followed
in future. No comment was offered about the recovery of the balance TI of
र₹ 3.50 lakh.

e Three security agencies were engaged during 2003-13 without assessing
requirement of guards, inviting tenders from the agencies and executing
agreements with them. A payment of हैं 1.59 crore was made to them
during that period. During discussion (January 2014), the Government
accepted the audit observation that security agencies were engaged without
inviting the tenders and executing agreements with them.

2.4.3. Internal control

Internal control is a process designed to provide reasonable assurance
regarding achievement of the objectives of an organization with regard to
efficiency and effectiveness of operations, compliance to rules and regulations
and sound financial reporting. The following were noticed.

e There was no internal audit wing in IERT, Allahabad during the period
covered in audit. As a result, the financial irregularities remained
undetected.

e The Local Funds Audit, Allahabad conducted audit of accounts of IERT
during 2003-12 and highlighted the issues of non-adjustment of TIs, non-
transfer of tuition fee to the Salary Bank Account etc. (Appendix-2.4.7).
The reports were issued during 2003-12. However, the compliance reports
were awaited (June 2013).

During discussion (January 2014), the Government accepted the observation.
2.4.4 Conclusion

The Government did not take any action for prescribing forms of accounts for
TERT even after 20 years of taking over the management. The Institute was
operating multiple bank accounts and the required periodical reconciliation
was not being done, resulting in un-reconciled differences. There were
instances of misuse and non-accountal of tuition fee. The Institute had opened
multiple cash books that were not being maintained properly as there were

ee)


Audit Report (Gener:

over writings, cuttings without authentication. Large number of TIs were
adjusted with delays and TIs were given without adjustment of the previous
one. Further, large number of TIs were lying unadjusted. Excess fee were
realised from the students of Degree and Diploma divisions of the Institute.
Excess reimbursement of fee from DSWO was claimed by the Director under
Post Matric Scholarship Scheme, resulting in excess reimbursement of the
scholarship fee by the DSWO. Contributions to the Contributory Provident
Funds were not transferred in respect of work charged staff of the Institute.
Financial statements were not maintained properly and the differences in the
financial data remained un-reconciled Therefore, the Financial statements did
not reflect the true and fair picture of the financial position of the Institute.
There was lack of Internal Control at the Government and the institute level.

PUBLIC W

KS DEPARTMENT

2.5 Operation of Plant & Machinery in Electrical and Mechanical
divisions
2.5.1 Introduction

Engineer-in-chief, Public Works Department (PWD), Uttar Pradesh, Lucknow
(E-in-C) entrusted (January 1984) operation of departmental plants and
machines to four Electrical and Mechanical (E&M) divisions!. Later, in
September 19902 , operation of light machines * with attached staff was
entrusted to the existing civil divisions while Heavy Plants and Machines*
(P&M) with attached staff was retained in all E&M divisions.

E&M divisions provide plants and machines alongwith operating staff to the
civil divisions on hire basis. The hire charges are fixed by E-in-C once in a
year between July and September. The hire charges for heavy plants &
machines and ninety per cent of hire charges for light machines are credited to
E&M divisions by civil divisions for major repairs of machines while civil
divisions retain 10 per cent of hire charges for petty repairs of light machines.

The audit of mechanical wing of E&M divisions was undertaken to assess
mainly the status of operation of Plants and Machines (P&Ms), recovery of
hire charges and management of Depreciation Reserve Fund (DRF).

Records of Chief Engineer (CE), all the four E&M circles and six®, out of 18
E&M divisions, were test-checked during April to July 2013 covering the
period 2008-13. An entry conference was held with CE on 10 April 2013
andex it conference with Principal Secretary, PWD was held on 16 January
2014. The Government’s replies have been suitably incorporated.

Audit Findings

Major audit findings on operation of plants and machines in E&M divisions
are discussed below:

2.5.2 Utilisation of Plants and Machines

The State Government ordered (October 2002) for optimum utilisation of
departmental plants and machineries. E-in-C, while reviewing the adherence
to the aforesaid Government’s order instructed (July 2009) to carry out the
works related to general maintenance, special repair and renewal of roads by
utilising departmental P&Ms. E-in-C, further, added (July 2009) that while

'B&M division, Lucknow (now absorbed in Mechanical division, Lucknow), E&M division, Varanasi, E&M division,
Moradabad and E &M division, Pithoragarh (Now in Uttarakhand),

> Scrutiny revealed that as per the decision of High Level Purchase
Committee’, Plant and Machines'’ amounting to = 41.58 crore were
procured, out of DRF, by the department between February 2007 and

underutilised. Of plants and machineries procured:

by the E-in-C. The shortfall of four out of these six plants was
84 per cent or above.

Thus, the procurement of these plants and machines was unwarranted and
without any need based assessment.

In reply, the Government accepted (January 2014) the facts and stated that
orders have been issued (December 2013) for utilisation of departmental
machines in renewal works. It was also stated that efforts would be made for
optimum utilisation of machines. As regards idle WMM plants, the
Government stated (January 2014) that the final reply will be given after
review of the matter. The fact remains that P&Ms were under utilised which
rendered the expenditure incurred on procurement alongwith overhead
charges largely unfruitful.

2.5.3 Recovery of hire charges

As per E-in-C’s order (September 1990) the civil divisions were to provide 90
per cent of hire charges to E&M divisions. However, scrutiny revealed that
during 2008-13, हें 62.36 crore was provided to E&M divisions as of March
2013 against the required हैं. 67.25crore. Thus, there was shortfall of = 4.89
crore in recovery of hire charges from the concerned civil divisions. In test-
checked divisions,® 8.81 crore, out of due% 9.52 crore, was provided to the six
E&M divisions as of March 2013.

Further, it was also noticed that the hire charges of % 7.25 crore pertaining to
period prior to August 2003 were neither credited nor accounted for in demand
of hire charges to be raised against the concerned civil divisions. CE informed
(April 2013) that the chances of recovery of = 7.25 crore were remote and was
hence shown separately in the report. Interestingly, the administrative control
of both the civil and E&M divisions vests in E-in-C.

In the exit conference (January 2014), the Government intimated that, out
of %7.25 crore, ® 5.93 crore remained as balance as of September 2013.

°The committee, headed by Principal Secretary, PWD was constituted for procurement of P&Ms under DRE

pertaining to periods prior to 2003 is not possible. The fact remains that
® 5.93 crore remained outstanding for adjustment for over 10 years now.
Further, the reply of recovery of ® 1.32 crore was not supported by
documentary evidence.

2.5.4 Management of Depreciation Reserve Fund

The Government created a Depreciation Reserve Fund (DRF) in March
2005for renewal or replacement of existing machines or equipments and
framed DRF Rules (March 2005) to regulate the fixed percentage of the
amount received from the Consolidated Fund of the State and any other fixed
percentage of money received for construction and maintenance works in
PWD.

During audit, we observed that = 120.61 crore was credited in the DRF while
र₹ 69.32 crore was spent during 2004-13. As of April 2013, € 51.29 crore was
lying in DRF. Thus, 43 per cent of DRF remained unutilised.

Further, according to DRF Rules, 2005, DRF was to be utilized only for
purchase of machines and equipments for renewal and replacement of
unserviceable machines or equipments, special repairs and purchase of
machines employing higher technology.

Scrutiny, however, revealed that in violation of DRF Rules, PWD
procured computers and photocopier machines'' worth = 51.85 lakh during
2008-09.Thus, money from the Fund was diverted for unauthorised purposes.

In reply, the Government accepted (January 2014) the facts.

2.5.5 Conclusion

Existing plants and machines were either non-utilised or under-utilised against
the norms. Under-realisation of hire charges from civil divisions needed to be
addressed to. Utilisation of funds from DRF should be as per rules. Further,
the department failed to ensure optimum utilisation of available plants and
machines in E&M divisions.
